Totally Ramified Maximal Tori and Bruhat-Tits theory
Stephen DeBacker

Abstract
. Suppose k k is a nonarchimedean local field, K K is a maximally unramified extension of k k , and 𝐆 \mathbf{G} is a connected reductive k k -group. If 𝐓 \mathbf{T} is a K K -minisotropic maximal k k -torus in 𝐆 \mathbf{G} , then we use Bruhat-Tits theory to describe the stable classes in the 𝐆 \mathbf{G} -orbit of 𝐓 \mathbf{T} , the rational classes in the 𝐆 \mathbf{G} -orbit of 𝐓 \mathbf{T} , and the k k -embeddings, up to rational conjugacy, into 𝐆 \mathbf{G} of 𝐓 \mathbf{T} . We also provide, via Bruhat-Tits theory, a complete and explicit description of: the rational conjugacy classes of K K -minisotropic maximal tame k k -tori in 𝐆 \mathbf{G} ; the stable classes of K K -minisotropic maximal tame k k -tori in 𝐆 \mathbf{G} ; and the k k -embeddings, up to rational conjugacy, into 𝐆 \mathbf{G} of a K K -minisotropic maximal tame k k -torus of 𝐆 \mathbf{G} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 20G25; Secondary 22E35

Introduction

Suppose
k
is a nonarchimedean local field,
K
is a maximally unramified extension of
k
, and
\mathbf{G}
is a connected reductive
k
-group. If
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
, then
\mathbf{T}^{K}
​, the maximal
K
-split torus in
\mathbf{T}
, is defined over
k
and
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal
K
-minisotropic
k
-torus in
\mathbf{L}=C_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{T}^{K})
, the centralizer in
\mathbf{G}
of
\mathbf{T}^{K}
. The group
\mathbf{L}
is an unramified twisted Levi subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
; that is,
\mathbf{L}
is a
k
-group that occurs as the Levi component of a parabolic
K
-subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
. Consequently, an approach to parameterizing the rational conjugacy classes of maximal tori in
\mathbf{G}
is to

•

parameterize the rational conjugacy classes of unramified twisted Levi subgroups of
\mathbf{G}
and

•

for each unramified twisted Levi subgroup
\mathbf{L}
of
\mathbf{G}
parameterize the
\mathbf{L}(k)
-conjugacy classes of
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori in
\mathbf{L}
.

This paper takes up the latter problem. The former problem is the subject of
[
13
]
.
Future work will take up the problem of parameterizing, via Bruhat-Tits theory, the rational classes of tame tori in a connected reductive
k
-group.

In an attempt to improve the exposition, we will assume in this introduction that
\mathbf{G}
is semisimple and
k
-split.

Recall that a
K
-torus
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{G}
is called
K
-anisotropic provided that
\X^{*}(\mathbf{T})
does not contain non-trivial characters that are defined over
K
. Such tori are sometimes also referred to as totally ramified
K
-tori. Let
G
denote the group of
K
-rational points of
\mathbf{G}
.

We begin by studying the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of
K
-anisotropic maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
. Whereas unramified tori interact very nicely with Bruhat-Tits theory (for example, the apartment of a maximal unramified torus in
\mathbf{G}
is always an affine subspace of the building of
G
which decomposes as a union of facets for
G
), the same is very much not true for totally ramified tori. However, as shown in Section
2
, we can always associate to
\mathbf{T}
, a totally ramified maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
, a unique point
x_{T}
in the reduced Bruhat-Tits building of
G
. Moreover, this point
x_{T}
has the remarkable property that its
G
-orbit intersects the closure of each alcove in the reduced building of
G
exactly once.
We then combine results of Haines and Rapoport
[
17
]
and Kottwitz
[
27
, Section 7]
to establish the following fundamental fact: the stabilizer in
G
of
x_{T}
can be written as the product of
T
and the parahoric subgroup attached to
x_{T}
.

Without some assumptions on
k
and
\mathbf{G}
, we cannot say much about
x_{T}
outside of groups of type
A_{n}
. However, under the assumption that the characteristic of
{\mathfrak{f}}
does not divide the order of the Weyl group, in Section
3
we explicitly describe the point attached to our totally ramified maximal
K
-torus
\mathbf{T}
of
\mathbf{G}
. This can be done because, under these tameness assumptions, the
G
-conjugacy classes of totally ramified maximal
K
-tori are parameterized by the elliptic conjugacy classes in the Weyl group. On the other hand, elliptic conjugacy classes in the Weyl group have unique (normalized) Kac coordinates, and these coordinates determine, up to
G
-conjugacy, the point
x_{T}
(see Lemma
3.5.1
).

Having described, albeit under modest tameness assumptions, the complete set of
G
-conjugacy classes of totally ramified maximal
K
-tori, in Section
4
we turn to the question of when such a
G
-conjugacy class contains a torus that is defined over
k
. We show that this will happen if and only if
\mathcal{O}_{T}
, the
G
-conjugacy class of
\mathbf{T}
, is stable under the action of
\Gal(K/k)
.

In Section
5
we again invoke a tameness assumption and use the parameterization of Section
3
to convert the criterion described above into an easy to check condition about Weyl group conjugacy classes. We then use this condition to show that (when
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-split) every
G
-orbit of totally ramified
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
contains a torus which is defined over
k
. See Corollary
5.4.4
for the precise statement.

When we assume that our totally ramified maximal torus
\mathbf{T}
is defined over
k
, we can prove several results about
\mathcal{O}_{T}^{k}
, the set of tori in
\mathcal{O}_{T}
that are defined over
k
. In particular, in Section
6
we establish natural parameterizations of both the
k
-stable classes in
\mathcal{O}_{T}^{k}
and the rational classes in
\mathcal{O}_{T}^{k}
. We also establish a natural parameterization of the
k
-embeddings, up to rational conjugacy, of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
. Recall that a
k
-embedding of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
is a
k
-morphism
f\colon\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
for which there exists
g\in G
such that
f(t)=gtg^{-1}
for all
t\in T
.
Along the way to establishing these results, in Section
6.3
we show that there is a natural identification of a quotient of
T
with the
\Omega
group of
G
. Recall that the
\Omega
group of
G
is the quotient of the stabilizer of an Iwahori subgroup by the Iwahori subgroup.

Our main result is Theorem
7.1.1
; it provides a complete accounting of the rational classes,
k
-stable classes, and
k
-embeddings of all tame totally ramified maximal
k
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
. We also work out several examples in Section
7
. In particular, up to
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy,
we enumerate all of the tame totally ramified
k
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
and describe all of their
k
-embeddings into
\mathbf{G}
for
\mathbf{G}
being
\SL_{n}
, unramified
\SU_{n}
,
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
,
{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
, or ramified
\SU_{3}
.
These calculations are quite challenging (for me). Although I’ve done all of the calculations multiple times, I am sure that errors remain. So, be cautious.

Motivated by the examples discussed above, in Section
8
we look at how the parameterizations introduced in Section
6
behave under certain isogenies.

Finally, in Section
9
we establish the existence of a totally ramified torus for which the associated point in the building is the barycenter of an alcove. We call such a torus a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter torus, and we explore some of its properties.

The results of Sections
2
and
3
hold under less restrictive hypotheses on
k
; see the discussion at the start of each of these sections.

1.
Notation, the Tits group, and some facts about tori

Suppose
k
is a local field with non-trivial discrete valuation
\nu
and residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
of characteristic
p
and cardinality
q
.
Let
\mathbf{G}
denote a connected reductive
k
-group. Let
\tilde{W}
denote the absolute Weyl group of
\mathbf{G}
. Let
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
denote the derived group of
\mathbf{G}
,
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}
the simply connected cover of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
the adjoint group of
\mathbf{G}
.
Let
\eta\colon\mathbf{G}_{\scon}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
denote the composition of the
k
-maps
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
. Let
\mathbf{Z}
denote the center of
\mathbf{G}
.

1.1.
Basic notation

Let
\bar{k}
denote a fixed separable closure of
k
, let
\nu
denote the unique extension of
\nu
to
\bar{k}
, and let
\varpi\in k
be a uniformizer. Let
K
denote the maximal unramified extension of
k
in
\bar{k}
, and let
\mathfrak{F}
denote the residue field of
K
; it is an algebraic closure of
{\mathfrak{f}}
. Let
R_{K}
denote the ring of integers in
K
. Let
I=\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
denote the inertial subgroup of
\Gal(\bar{k}/k)
. Let
{\Fro}
be a topological generator for
\Gal(K/k)\cong\Gal(\bar{k}/k)/I
, which we identify with
\Gal(\mathfrak{F}/{\mathfrak{f}})
.
We suppose that
{\Fro}^{-1}(x)=x^{q}
for all
x\in\mathfrak{F}
. Choose a lift of
{\Fro}
to an element, which we will also call
{\Fro}
, of
\Gal(\bar{k}/k)
. Let
L
denote the completion of
K
, and let
R_{L}
denote the ring of integers in
L
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
denote a maximal
K
-split
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
that contains a maximal
k
-split torus of
\mathbf{G}
; such a torus exists and is unique up to rational conjugacy (see
[
34
, Theorem 6.1]
or in
[
13
, Theorem 3.4.1]
take an unramified torus corresponding to a pair of the form
(F,\mathsf{T})\in I^{m}
with
F
an alcove).
Since
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-quasi-split, there exists a Borel
K
-subgroup
\mathbf{B}
that contains
\mathbf{A}
, and hence
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}=C_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{A})
is a maximally
K
-split
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
that is contained in
\mathbf{B}
. Let

\Phi=\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A})
denote the set of roots of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{A}
and
\Pi=\Pi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
the set of simple roots with respect to
\mathbf{G}
,
\mathbf{A}
, and
\mathbf{B}
. Similarly,
let
\tilde{\Phi}=\Phi(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
denote the set of roots of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{G}
and
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and
\tilde{\Pi}=\Pi(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B})
the set of simple roots with respect to
\mathbf{G}
,
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
, and
\mathbf{B}
.

Let
\tilde{K}
denote the splitting field over
K
of
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
.

If
\mathbf{H}
is an algebraic
K
-group, then we let
H
denote the group of
K
-points of
\mathbf{H}
, and we let
\tilde{H}
denote the group of
\tilde{K}
points of
\mathbf{H}
.
If
\mathsf{H}
is an
\mathfrak{F}
-group, we will often denote the group of
\mathfrak{F}
-points of
\mathsf{H}
by
\mathsf{H}
as well.

We let
k_{\dtame}
denote the maximal tame extension of
k
in
\bar{k}
, and we let
\sigma
denote a topological generator of
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is a group and
x,y\in\mathcal{G}
, then
{{}^{x}\!}y=xyx^{-1}
.
If
\tau
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{G}
, then
two elements
x,y\in\mathcal{G}
are said to be
\tau
-conjugate (in
\mathcal{G}
) provided that there exists
g\in\mathcal{G}
such that
g^{-1}x\tau(g)=y
.

If
X
and
Y
are sets,
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a function,
A\subset X
, and
B\subset Y
, then
f[A]\subset Y
denotes the image of
A
under
f
while
f^{-1}[B]\subset X
denotes the preimage of
B
.

If
F
is a field,
\mathbf{H}
is an
F
-group, and
\tau
is an
F
-automorphism of
\mathbf{H}
, then we let

\Fix_{\tau}(\mathbf{H})
denote the
\tau
-fixed points in
\mathbf{H}
and we let
\mathbf{H}^{\tau}
denote
\Fix_{\tau}(\mathbf{H})^{\circ}
, the connected component of
\Fix_{\tau}(\mathbf{H})
. Both
\Fix_{\tau}(\mathbf{H})
and
\mathbf{H}^{\tau}
are
F
-groups. An
F
-homomorphism between reductive
F
-groups
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}
is called an isogeny provided that it is surjective with finite central kernel.

1.2.
Notation for Bruhat-Tits theory

Let
\Psi=\Psi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A},K,\nu)
denote the set of affine roots of
\mathbf{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{A}
,
K
, and
\nu
. For
\psi\in\Psi
we let
\dot{\psi}\in\Phi
denote its gradient. The elements of
\Psi
define the facet structure on the apartment
\mathcal{A}(A)=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{A},K)
. Fix a
{\Fro}
-stable alcove
C
in
\mathcal{A}(A)
and let
\Delta=\Delta(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A},K,\nu,C)
denote the set of simple affine roots in
\Psi
determined by
C
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\Pi=\Pi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is a subset of
\{\dot{\psi}\,|\,\psi\in\Delta\}
.

For each facet
H
in the Bruhat-Tits building
{\mathcal{B}}(G)={\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G},K)
of
G
we let
G_{H,0}
denote the parahoric subgroup attached to
H
and we let
G_{H,0^{+}}
denote the pro-unipotent radical of
G_{H,0}
. The quotient
G_{H,0}/G_{H,0^{+}}
is the group of
\mathfrak{F}
-points of a connected reductive
\mathfrak{F}
-group which we denote by
\mathsf{G}_{H}
.

Suppose
H
is a facet in
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{A},K)
such that
H\subset\bar{C}
.
Let
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}_{H}
denote the torus in
\mathsf{G}_{H}
corresponding to the image of the parahoric subgroup
A_{0}=A\cap G_{H,0}
of
A
in
\mathsf{G}_{H}
. (We may drop the subscript
H
since these tori are independent of the choice of
H
in
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{A},K)
.) Let
\mathsf{B}^{C}_{H}
denote the Borel subgroup of
\mathsf{G}_{H}
corresponding to
G_{C,0}/G_{H,0^{+}}
. Note that
\mathsf{A}
is a maximal
{\mathfrak{f}}
-torus in
\mathsf{B}^{C}_{H}
, and hence in
\mathsf{G}_{H}
. Note that
\X^{*}(\mathbf{A})
may be canonically identified with
\X^{*}(\mathsf{A}_{H})
.

Let
F_{0}
denote the unique facet contained in the closure of
\bar{C}
such that (a)
F_{0}
is special and (b) the set of simple roots determined by
\mathsf{G}_{F_{0}}
,
\mathsf{A}
, and
\mathsf{B}^{C}_{F_{0}}
corresponds to
\Pi
. See
[
35
, Section 2.10]
and
[
36
, Section 7.1]
for the existence of such a facet when
\mathbf{G}
is absolutely simple and simply connected; since the existence of such a minimal facet is independent of isogeny class, the general case follows by considering the almost simple factors of
\mathbf{G}
. The uniqueness of such a facet follows from condition (b). Note that
F_{0}
will be a special facet over the splitting field of
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
, and so is absolutely special in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G},K)
; see
[
18
, Section 5]
or
[
24
, Appendix G]

for more about absolutely special facets.

1.3.
A realization of the Tits group

Recall that
\tilde{K}
denotes the splitting field of
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
over
K
. Set
\tilde{\Gamma}=\Gal(\tilde{K}/K)
and
\tilde{G}=\mathbf{G}(\tilde{K})
,
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}={\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}(\tilde{K})
, and
\tilde{B}=\mathbf{B}(\tilde{K})
. We may and do identify
\tilde{W}
with
N_{\tilde{G}}({\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})/{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. For a facet
F\subset{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{G})
, let
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F}
denote the connected reductive
\mathfrak{F}
-group whose group of
\mathfrak{F}
-rational points is
\tilde{G}_{F,0}/\tilde{G}_{F,0^{+}}
.
Let
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
denote the
\mathfrak{F}
-torus in
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F_{0}}
whose group of
\mathfrak{F}
-points coincides with the image of the parahoric subgroup
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}={\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\cap\tilde{G}_{F_{0},0}
of
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
in
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F_{0}}(\mathfrak{F})
. Similarly, Let
\tilde{\mathsf{B}}
denote the Borel
\mathfrak{F}
-group in
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F_{0}}
whose group of
\mathfrak{F}
-points coincides with the image of
\tilde{B}\cap\tilde{G}_{F_{0},0}
in
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F_{0}}(\mathfrak{F})
.
Choose a pinning
(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B},\{X_{a}\}_{a\in\tilde{\Pi}})
that is
\tilde{\Gamma}
-stable and compatible with
F_{0}
. When we say the pinning is compatible with
F_{0}
, we mean that
X_{a}\in\Lie(\tilde{G}_{F_{0},0})
for all
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
and if
\bar{X}_{a}
denotes the image of
X_{a}
in
\Lie(\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F_{0}})
, then
(\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F_{0}},\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\tilde{\mathsf{B}},\{\bar{X}_{a}\}_{a\in\tilde{\Pi}})
is a pinning of
(\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{F_{0}},\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\tilde{\mathsf{B}})
.

For
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
we let
\mathbf{G}_{a}
denote the
\tilde{K}
-subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
generated by the root groups
\mathbf{U}_{a}
and
\mathbf{U}_{-a}
in
\mathbf{G}
and set
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{a}=\mathbf{G}_{a}\cap{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. Since for all nontrivial
u\in\mathbf{U}_{a}(\tilde{K})
, the intersection
u\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})u\cap N_{\mathbf{G}_{a}(\tilde{K})}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{a})
has cardinality one, the pinning
(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B},\{X_{a}\}_{a\in\tilde{\Pi}})
uniquely identifies an element
n_{a}\in N_{\mathbf{G}_{a}(\tilde{K})}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{a})\leq N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
. We have
n_{a}^{2}=\check{a}(-1)\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
where
\check{a}\in\X_{*}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
is the coroot associated to
a
. Suppose
\tau\leq{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
denotes the elementary abelian two-group generated by
\{\check{a}(-1)\,|\,a\in\tilde{\Pi}\}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
denotes the (finite) subgroup of
N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
generated by
\{n_{a}\,|\,a\in\tilde{\Pi}\}
.
Note that since our pinning is
\tilde{\Gamma}
-stable, we have that
\tilde{\Gamma}
acts on both
\tau
and
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
.
If
\pi\colon N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})\rightarrow\tilde{W}
denotes the usual projection, then thanks to
[
47
]
we have an exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\tau\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{W}\longrightarrow 1.

of finite groups with
\tilde{\Gamma}
-action. Note that
\tau
is a subgroup of
\tilde{G}_{F_{0},0}
, and, since our pinning is compatible with
F_{0}
, we have that
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
is also a subgroup of
\tilde{G}_{F_{0},0}
. The group
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
is called the Tits group (with respect to our chosen pinning).

Remark 1.3.1
.

Since the root groups
\mathbf{U}_{a}
for
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
belong to
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\leq\tilde{G}^{\prime}_{F_{0},0}\leq\tilde{G}^{\prime}
.

1.4.
Facts about tori

We recall some facts about tori that will be used later. Recall that every
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
splits over a separable extension
[
30
, Corollary 12.19]
.

1.4.1.
On the structure of parahoric subgroups of tori

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a
k
-torus. Since
\mathbf{T}
is abelian and any two maximal
K
-split tori in
\mathbf{T}
are
T=\mathbf{T}(K)
-conjugate, there is a unique maximal
K
-split
K
-torus,
\mathbf{T}^{K}
, in
\mathbf{T}
. Since it is unique and
\mathbf{T}
is a
k
-torus,
\mathbf{T}^{K}
is a
k
-torus as well. Recall that
R_{K}
denotes the ring of integers in
K
. The maximal bounded subgroup of
T^{K}
is
T^{K}_{0}=\mathbf{T}^{K}(R_{K})
, and
T^{K}_{0}
is
the
parahoric subgroup of
T^{K}
.

Let
T_{0}
denote the parahoric subgroup of
T
and let
T_{0^{+}}
denote its pro-unipotent radical. The quotient
T_{0}/T_{0^{+}}
is isomorphic to the
\mathfrak{F}
-points of an
{\mathfrak{f}}
-torus
\overline{\mathsf{T}}
. Moreover,
T^{K}_{0}=T_{0}\cap T^{K}
and the image of
T^{K}_{0}
in
T_{0}/T_{0^{+}}
may be identified with the
\mathfrak{F}
-points of
\overline{\mathsf{T}}
. From this we conclude that
T_{0}=T^{K}_{0}T_{0^{+}}.

Since
T_{0}
,
T^{K}_{0}
, and
T_{0^{+}}
are
{\Fro}
-stable and
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T^{K}_{0}\cap T_{0^{+}})
is trivial, we have
T_{0}^{{\Fro}}=({T^{K}_{0}})^{{\Fro}}\,T_{0^{+}}^{{\Fro}}
.

Remark 1.4.1
.

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}
denote the Lie algebra of
\mathbf{T}
. If
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split, we can (canonically) write
{\mathfrak{t}}=\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}}\oplus\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}}^{\perp}
where
\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}}
is the Lie algebra of
{\mathbf{T}}^{K}
and
\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}}^{\perp}=(C_{{\mathfrak{g}}}(\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}}))^{\prime}\cap{\mathfrak{t}}
, where
(C_{{\mathfrak{g}}}(\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}}))^{\prime}
denotes the derived Lie algebra of
C_{{\mathfrak{g}}}(\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}})
. If
\mathbf{G}
is not
K
-split, then we need to replace
\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}}
with the center of
C_{{\mathfrak{g}}}(\bar{{\mathfrak{t}}})
.

1.4.2.
Splitting fields of tori in quasi-split groups

Suppose
F
is a field and
\mathbf{H}
is an
F
-quasi-split group. Let
(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{S})
be a Borel-torus pair for
\mathbf{H}
; so
\mathbf{B}
is a Borel
F
-subgroup of
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{S}
is a maximal
F
-torus of
\mathbf{H}
that is contained in
\mathbf{B}
.

Lemma 1.4.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal
F
-torus in
\mathbf{H}
. If
E
is the splitting field of
\mathbf{T}
, then
\mathbf{S}
is
E
-split.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{T}
is
E
-split, there exists a Borel
E
-subgroup
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{H}
such that
\mathbf{T}\leq\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
. Since all Borel
E
-subgroups of
\mathbf{G}
are
\mathbf{H}(E)
-conjugate
[
5
, Theorem 20.9 (i)]
, there exists
h\in\mathbf{H}(E)
such that
{{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}\leq\mathbf{B}
. Since
{{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{S}
are maximal
E
-tori in
\mathbf{B}
, they are
\mathbf{B}(E)
-conjugate
[
5
, Proposition 20.5]
. Consequently, since
\mathbf{S}
is
\mathbf{H}(E)
-conjugate to the
E
-split torus
\mathbf{T}
, we conclude that
\mathbf{S}
is
E
-split as well.
∎

2.
Points in the reduced building attached to
K
-minisotropic maximal tori

In this section we loosen our restrictions on
k
: it can be any complete field with nontrivial discrete valuation
\nu
and perfect residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
such that
K
is strictly Henselian and has cohomological dimension
\leq 1
.

Recall from §
1.2
that
C
is
an alcove in the apartment
\mathcal{A}(A)
in
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
.
Let
C^{\prime}
denote the image of
C
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
, the reduced building of
G
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)
will denote the image of
\mathcal{A}(A)
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
.

Definition 2.0.1
.

A
K
-torus
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{G}
is said to be
K
-minisotropic provided that
\mathbf{X}^{*}(\mathbf{T}/\mathbf{Z})^{\Gal(\bar{k}/K)}
is trivial.

In this section we show that to every
K
-minisotropic maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
we can associate a unique point in the closure of the alcove
C^{\prime}
. We then describe the stabilizer of this point in Lemma
2.3.1
(see also Remark
2.3.3
).

Under the assumption that the torus splits over a tame extension, we describe this point explicitly in Section
3
.

2.1.
Results on
G
-conjugacy

An element
\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
is called
semisimple
provided that it belongs to a maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
. The element
\gamma
is said to be
strongly regular semisimple
provided that there exists a maximal torus
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{G}
such that

•

\gamma\in\mathbf{T}(\bar{k})
,

•

\alpha(\gamma)\neq 1
for all
\alpha\in\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{T})
, and

•

the stabilizer of
\gamma
in the Weyl group of
\mathbf{T}
is trivial.

The set
\mathbf{G}^{\srss}(\bar{k})
of strongly regular semisimple elements in
\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
is both dense and open. In fact, for any maximal
k
-torus
\mathbf{T}
of
\mathbf{G}
the set
\mathbf{T}(\bar{k})\cap\mathbf{G}^{\srss}(\bar{k})
of strongly regular semisimple elements in
\mathbf{T}(\bar{k})
is both dense and open. Since
\mathbf{T}
is unirational, this implies that
\mathbf{T}(k)
contains a strongly regular semisimple element. If
\gamma\in\mathbf{G}^{\srss}(\bar{k})
, then the centralizer of
\gamma
is a maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
. An element that satisfies the first two conditions, but not the third, is said to be
regular semisimple
.

Lemma 2.1.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{H}
is a connected reductive
K
-group. Two
elements of
H^{\srss}=\mathbf{H}^{\srss}(K)
are
H
-conjugate if and only if
they are
\mathbf{H}(\bar{k})
-conjugate.

Proof.

Suppose
x,y\in H=\mathbf{H}(K)
are strongly regular semisimple. It will be enough to show that if there exists
g\in\mathbf{H}(\bar{k})
for which
{{}^{g}\!}x=y
, then there exists
h\in H
for which
{{}^{h}\!}x=y
. Since
x
is strongly regular semisimple, its centralizer is a maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{H}
, call it
\mathbf{T}
. For all
\gamma\in\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
we have
{{}^{\gamma(g)^{-1}g}\!}x=x
, hence
g^{-1}\gamma(g)\in\mathbf{T}(\bar{k})
. Thanks to
[
6
, Section 8.6]
, we have that
\cohom^{1}(K,\mathbf{T})
is trivial. Hence there exists
t\in\mathbf{T}(\bar{k})
such that
\gamma(gt)=gt
for all
\gamma\in\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
. Set
h=gt
.
∎

Remark 2.1.2

(Loren Spice)
.

Suppose that
k^{\text{alg}}/\bar{k}
is an algebraic closure of
\bar{k}
. Two semisimple elements
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
are
\mathbf{G}(k^{\text{alg}})
-conjugate if and only if they are
\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
-conjugate. Indeed, since every
\bar{k}
-torus is
\bar{k}
-split
[
30
, Corollary 12.19]
, we may, and do, assume, after conjugating by an element of
\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
, that both
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
belong to the group of
\bar{k}
-points of a
\bar{k}
-split, maximal torus
\mathbf{S}
in
\mathbf{G}_{\bar{k}}
.
Suppose that
g\in\mathbf{G}(k^{\text{alg}})
satisfies
\Int(g)\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
. Since both
\mathbf{S}
and
\ \Int(g^{-1})\mathbf{S}
are maximal
k^{\text{alg}}
-split tori in
\mathbf{H}:=C_{\mathbf{G}}(\gamma)^{\circ}
, there exists
h\in\mathbf{H}(k^{\text{alg}})
such that
\Int(g^{-1})\mathbf{S}=\Int(h)\mathbf{S}
.
Then
\Int(gh)\mathbf{S}_{k^{\text{alg}}}=\mathbf{S}_{k^{\text{alg}}}
, so that
gh
belongs to
N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})(k^{\text{alg}})
. Since
N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})
is smooth with identity component
C_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})=\mathbf{S}
, we have that there is some
n\in N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})(\bar{k})
whose image in the Weyl group
(N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{S})/\mathbf{S})(k^{\text{alg}})
is the same as the image of
gh
; i.e., so that
n
belongs to
gh\mathbf{S}(k^{\text{alg}})
. In particular,
\Int(n)\gamma
equals
\Int(gh)\gamma=\Int(g)\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}_{i}
is a maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
. We say that
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is
K
-stably conjugate
to
\mathbf{T}_{2}
in
\mathbf{G}
provided that there exists
h\in\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
such that
\Ad(h)\colon\mathbf{T}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}_{2}
is a
K
-morphism.

Corollary 2.1.4
.

Suppose
\mathbf{H}
is a connected reductive
K
-group. Two maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{H}
are
H
-conjugate if and only if they are
K
-stably-conjugate in
\mathbf{H}
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{H}
, then
T
contains a strongly regular semisimple element. The result follows from Lemma
2.1.1
.
∎

Corollary 2.1.5
.

Two maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
are
G
-conjugate if and only if the corresponding maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}_{*}
are
G_{*}
-conjugate. Here
*
is either
\scon
or
\ad
.

Proof.

The canonical
k
-maps
\mathbf{G}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
and
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\ad}

establish natural bijective correspondences among (a) the set of maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
, (b) the set of maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
, and (c) the set of maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}
.
Moreover, under these correspondences two maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
are
K
-stably-conjugate if and only if the corresponding maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}_{*}
are
K
-stably-conjugate. Here
*
is either
\scon
or
\ad
.

The result follows from Corollary
2.1.4
.
∎

If we assume that
K
has characteristic zero, then there are similar results on the Lie algebra level.

Lemma 2.1.6
.

Suppose
K
has characteristic zero.
Suppose
\mathbf{H}
is a connected reductive
K
-group with Lie algebra
{\mathbf{\mathfrak{h}}}
. Two regular semisimple elements of
{\mathbf{\mathfrak{h}}}(K)
are
H
-conjugate if and only if they are
\mathbf{H}(\bar{k})
-conjugate.

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to that of Lemma
2.1.1
.
∎

Let
{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}
denote the Lie algebra of
\mathbf{G}
, and denote by
{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}
the Lie algebra of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
the derived group of
\mathbf{G}
. When
K
has characteristic zero, we may and do identify
the Lie algebras of
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}
and
\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
with
{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}
.

Corollary 2.1.7
.

Suppose
K
has characteristic zero. Suppose
X_{1},X_{2}\in{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}(K)
are regular semisimple. The following are equivalent:

•

X_{1}
is
G
-conjugate to
X_{2}
.

•

X_{1}
is
G_{sc}
-conjugate to
X_{2}
.

•

X_{1}
is
G_{ad}
-conjugate to
X_{2}
. ∎

Since, in characteristic zero, every maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
or
\mathbf{G}_{*}
arises as the centralizer of a regular semisimple element of
{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}(k)
, we have that a maximal
k
-torus
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{G}
corresponds to the maximal
k
-torus
\mathbf{T}_{*}
in
\mathbf{G}_{*}
provided that there exists a regular semisimple
X\in{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}(k)
such that
\mathbf{T}=C_{\mathbf{G}}(X)
and
\mathbf{T}_{*}=C_{\mathbf{G}_{*}}(X)
. This correspondence is well defined since
C_{\mathbf{G}}(C_{{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}}(X))=C_{\mathbf{G}}(X^{\prime})
and
C_{\mathbf{G}_{*}}(C_{{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}}(X))=C_{\mathbf{G}_{*}}(X^{\prime})
for all regular semisimple
X^{\prime}
belonging to the Cartan subalgebra
C_{{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\prime}}(X)(K)
. Thus, in characteristic zero Corollary
2.1.7
gives another proof of Corollary
2.1.5
.

2.2.
Points in the reduced building associated to
K
-minisotropic maximal tori of
\mathbf{G}

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
.

Let
T=\mathbf{T}(K)
.
We may associate to
\mathbf{T}
a point
x_{T}
in the reduced building of
G
as follows: if
E
is a Galois extension of
K
over which
\mathbf{T}
splits and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
denotes the apartment of
\mathbf{T}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},E)
, then the set of
\Gal(E/K)
-fixed points of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},E)
is a point. If
E/K
is not tame, it is possible that this point may not lie in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
. However, thanks to the non-positive curvature of buildings, there is a unique closest point,
x_{T}
, in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)={\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},K)
to the
\Gal(E/K)
-fixed point of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Moreover, thanks to uniqueness, the point
x_{T}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
is fixed by
T
.
Let
F
denote the facet in the building of
G
whose image under the projection to the reduced building contains
x_{T}
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

Since
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
is independent of the isogeny type of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, we have
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)={\mathcal{B}}(G_{\scon})={\mathcal{B}}(G_{\ad})
. Thanks to Corollary
2.1.5
, the
G
-orbit of
x_{T}
is equal to the
G_{*}
-orbit of
x_{T}
where
*
is either
\scon
or
\ad
. That is, the subset
G\cdot x_{T}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
is independent of the isogeny type of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

The set
G\cdot x_{T}\cap\bar{C}^{\prime}
has cardinality one.

Proof.

Thanks to Corollary
2.1.5
, the
G
-orbit of
x_{T}
is equal to the
G_{\scon}
-orbit of
x_{T}
.
Since
\bar{C}^{\prime}
is a fundamental domain for the action of
G_{\scon}
on
{\mathcal{B}}(G_{\scon})
, the result follows.
∎

2.3.
Some consequences of results of Haines and Rapoport and of Kottwitz

In
[
27
, Section 7]
Kottwitz shows that there is a functorial surjective homomorphism
\kappa_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow\X^{*}(\hat{Z}(\mathbf{G})^{I})
. Here
I
denotes
\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
, and
\hat{Z}(\mathbf{G})
denotes the center of the dual group of
\mathbf{G}
. If
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{G})_{I}
denotes the
I
-coinvariants of the fundamental group of
\mathbf{G}
, then
\X^{*}(\hat{Z}(\mathbf{G})^{I})\cong\pi_{1}(\mathbf{G})_{I}
and we have a functorial surjective homomorphism
\kappa_{G}\colon G\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathbf{G})_{I}
(see
[
23
, Section 3.3]
). In
[
17
, Proposition 3]
Haines and Rapoport show that for any facet
F^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
the restriction of
\kappa_{G}
to
\Stab_{G}(F^{\prime})
has kernel
G_{F^{\prime},0}
.

These results have many interesting consequences. As an example, which can be vastly generalized as in
[
23
, Corollary 3.3.1]
, we have
\eta[(G_{\scon})_{x,0}]\leq G_{x,0}
where
\eta\colon\mathbf{G}_{\scon}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
denotes the composition
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
and
x
is any point in
{\mathcal{B}}(G_{\scon})={\mathcal{B}}(G^{\prime})={\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
. In this section, we record some additional consequences.

The following result plays a fundamental role throughout the remainder of this paper.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
. Let
x_{T}
denote the point in the reduced building of
G
attached to
T
, and recall that
F
denotes the facet in the building of
G
whose image under the projection to the reduced building contains
x_{T}
. We have

\Stab_{G}(F)=TG_{F,0}.

Remark 2.3.2
.

Recall that the building of
T
does not always embed into that of
G
.
In the proof below we
use that
F
is
T
-stable.

Proof.

For any maximal
K
-torus
\mathbf{S}
in
\mathbf{G}
, the map
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{S})_{I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\rightarrow}}\pi_{1}(\mathbf{G})_{I}
is surjective.
Consequently, we have that for all
g\in\Stab_{G}(F)
there is a
t\in T
such that
\kappa_{G}(g)=\iota(\kappa_{T}(t))
. We then have
t^{-1}g\in\ker(\kappa_{G})
. Thus,
t^{-1}g\in\Stab_{G}(F)\cap\ker(\kappa_{G})=G_{F,0}
.
∎

Remark 2.3.3
.

This result can be restated as:

\Stab_{G}(x_{T})=TG_{x_{T},0}.

Corollary 2.3.4
.

The map
T\rightarrow\Stab_{G}(F)/G_{F,0}
is surjective with kernel
\eta[T_{\scon}]T_{0}
. Here
\mathbf{T}_{\scon}
is the torus in
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}
corresponding to
\mathbf{T}

Proof.

The surjectivity of the map follows from Lemma
2.3.1
.
Since taking coinvariants is right exact, the exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}_{\scon})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathbf{G})\longrightarrow 1

yields

\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}_{\scon})_{I}{\longrightarrow}\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I}\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathbf{G})_{I}\longrightarrow 1.

Using the results of Kottwitz and Haines and Rapoport discussed above and the fact that
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{S})=\X_{*}(\mathbf{S})
for any
K
-torus
\mathbf{S}
, we conclude that we have an exact sequence

T_{sc}/(T_{sc})_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\eta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T/T_{0}\longrightarrow\Stab_{G}(F)/G_{F,0}\longrightarrow 1.

The result follows.
∎

Lemma 2.3.5
.

Suppose
g\in G^{\prime}
and
y\in{\mathcal{B}}(G^{\prime})
is special. If
g\in\Stab_{G^{\prime}}(y)
, then
g\in G^{\prime}_{y,0}
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{g}
denote the image of
g
in
G_{\ad}
and let
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\ad}
denote the maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
corresponding to
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. We first show that
\bar{g}\in(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
. Since
\bar{g}\in\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(y)
, it is enough to show that
\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(y)\subset(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
. Suppose
h\in\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(y)
. Since
(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
acts transitively on the apartments that contain
y
, there exists
k\in(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
such that
{{}^{kh}\!}{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\ad}={\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\ad}
; that is,
kh\in N_{G_{\ad}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\ad})
. Since
y
is special, there exists
j\in(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
that has the same image in
\tilde{W}
as
kh
. Thus
j^{-1}kh\in{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\ad}\cap\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(y)
. However, from
[
10
, Propositions 4.4.3 and 4.4.16]
we have
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\ad}\cap\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(y)=({A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{\ad})_{0}\leq(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
.

Since
(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
is the kernel of the restriction to
{\Stab_{y}(G_{\ad})}
of
\kappa_{G_{\ad}}
,
G^{\prime}_{y,0}
is the kernel of the restriction to
\Stab_{y}(G^{\prime})
of
\kappa_{G^{\prime}}
, and
the map from
\X^{*}(\hat{Z}(\mathbf{G}^{\prime})^{I})
to
\X^{*}(\hat{Z}(\mathbf{G}_{\ad})^{I})

is injective, by functoriality we conclude that since
\bar{g}\in(G_{\ad})_{y,0}
we must have
g\in G^{\prime}_{y,0}
.
∎

Remark 2.3.6
.

Suppose
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{L}
are connected semisimple
k
-groups and
\rho\colon\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}
is an isogeny. Suppose
x\in{\mathcal{B}}(H)={\mathcal{B}}(L)
.
We have that
\rho
carries
\Fix_{H}(x)
into
\Fix_{L}(x)
and
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[\Fix_{L}(x)]
, the preimage of
\Fix_{L}(x)
under
\res_{H}\rho
, is a subgroup of
\Fix_{H}(x)
. Moreover, since the map from
\X^{*}(\hat{Z}(\mathbf{H})^{I})
to
\X^{*}(\hat{Z}(\mathbf{L})^{I})
is injective, using the results of Kottwitz and Haines-Rapoport, we conclude
that
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[L_{x,0}]
is
H_{x,0}
. That is, for all
h\in\Stab_{H}(x)
we have
h\in H_{x,0}
if and only if
\rho(h)\in L_{x,0}
.

3.
Points in the reduced building attached to tame maximal
K
-tori of
\mathbf{G}

As in Section
2
, we loosen our restrictions on
k
: it can be any complete field with nontrivial discrete valuation
\nu
and perfect residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
such that either (a)
{\mathfrak{f}}
is finite or (b)
k
is strictly Henselian and quasi-finite. Note that in the latter case we have that
k=K
. In either case, we have that
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
has a topological generator
\sigma
,
K
is strictly Henselian, and
K
has cohomological dimension
\leq 1
(see
[
42
, II.3.3.c)]
for case (a) and
[
44
, XIII, Props. 3 and 5]
for case (b)). If
{\mathfrak{f}}
has characteristic zero, then we set
p=0
.

A
K
-torus
\mathbf{T}
of
\mathbf{G}
will be called
\emph{tame}
provided that
\mathbf{T}
is
k_{\dtame}
-split. A main result of this section is the explicit identification, up to
G
-conjugacy, of the point
x_{T}
attached to a tame
K
-minisotropic maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
.

3.1.
Tame elements in Weyl groups

We let
x_{0}\in\bar{C}^{\prime}
denote the image of
F_{0}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
. (The facet
F_{0}\subset\bar{C}
was introduced in Section
1.2
.)

If
\mathbf{G}
contains a tame maximal torus, then from Lemma
1.4.2
we know that
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
splits over a tame extension. Thus, in this section we assume that
\tilde{K}
, the splitting field of
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
over
K
, is a tame extension of
K
. Under this assumption there exists
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that
\tilde{K}={k_{\dtame}}^{\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}}
,
\tilde{K}^{\sigma}=K
, and
\tilde{\Gamma}
is the cyclic group
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)/\Gal(k_{\dtame}/\tilde{K})
. We let
\bar{\sigma}
denote the image of
\sigma
in this quotient. Denote by
\Aut(\tilde{\Pi})
the automorphism group of the Dynkin diagram associated to
\tilde{\Pi}
and let
\tau_{\bar{\sigma}}
denote the image of
\bar{\sigma}
in
\Aut(\tilde{\Pi})
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

An element
w\in\tilde{W}
is called
tame
provided that
p
does not divide the order of
(w,\tau_{\bar{\sigma}})
in
\tilde{W}\rtimes\Aut(\tilde{\Pi})
.
An element
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
will be called
tame
provided that its image, under the projection map
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\rightarrow\tilde{W}
, is tame.

Remark 3.1.2
.

Since the order of
\Aut(\tilde{\Pi})
always divides the order of
\tilde{W}
, we conclude from Cauchy’s theorem that every element of
\tilde{W}
is tame if and only if
p
does not divide the order of
\tilde{W}
.

Lemma 3.1.3
.

Suppose
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
. We have
n
is tame if and only if
p
does not divide the order of
n\bar{\sigma}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\rtimes\tilde{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{K}
is a tame extension of
K
. The kernel of the projection map from
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\rtimes\tilde{\Gamma}
to
\tilde{W}\rtimes\Aut(\tilde{\Pi})
is of the form
\tau\rtimes\langle\bar{\sigma}^{j}\rangle
where
\tau\subset{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
is an elementary abelian two group and
j
divides the order of
\tilde{\Gamma}
.
Since
p
does not divide the order of
\tilde{\Gamma}
and two divides the order of every nontrivial Weyl group, the result follows.
∎

Definition 3.1.4
.

Suppose
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
is tame, and let
\ell
denote the order of
n\bar{\sigma}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\rtimes\tilde{\Gamma}
.
Since
p
does not divide
\ell
, we can form the tame degree
\ell
extension
K_{n}={k_{\dtame}}^{\sigma^{\ell}}
of
K
; note that
\tilde{K}\leq K_{n}
.

3.2.
On the
\sigma
-conjugacy classes of tame elements of
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}

The results of this section will feel familiar to those who have studied conjugacy classes in disconnected groups. I was influenced by the presentation in
[
31
]
.

Let

{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}
denote the parahoric subgroup of
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0^{+}}
its pro-unipotent radical. Similarly, let
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}
denote the parahoric subgroup of
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0^{+}}
its pro-unipotent radical. Since
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
is
\tilde{K}
-split, we have
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}={\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\cap\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

If
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
is tame, then there exists a
\tilde{K}
-Borel-torus pair
(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
such that

•

n\sigma(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
and

•

x_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\tilde{K})
.

Remark 3.2.2
.

Since the automorphism
n\sigma
fixes a Borel-torus pair, it is an example of a quasi-semisimple (or quass) automorphism.

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{K}
is a tame extension of
K
.

Choose tame
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
. Let
\ell
denote the order of
n\bar{\sigma}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\rtimes\tilde{\Gamma}
.
Define the continuous 1-cocycle
\tau\in Z^{1}(\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K),\mathbf{G}(k_{\dtame}))
by

\tau(\sigma^{j})=N_{j}^{\sigma}(n):=n\sigma(n)\sigma^{2}(n)\cdots\sigma^{j-2}(n)\sigma^{j-1}(n).

Since the surjection
\Gal(\bar{k}/K)\rightarrow\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
yields
\cohom^{1}(\Gal(\bar{k}/K),\mathbf{G})\cong\cohom^{1}(\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K),\mathbf{G}(k_{\dtame}))
(see
[
42
, I.5.8]
and use the fact that
k_{\dtame}
has cohomological dimension
\leq 1
), the 1-cocycle
\tau
defines a twisted
K
-group, which we denote by
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}
. In fact, since
K
has cohomological dimension
\leq 1
, the groups
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}
and
\mathbf{G}
are isomorphic as
K
-groups.

Since
(\Ad(n)\circ\sigma)^{\ell}=\sigma^{\ell}
, we have
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}(K_{n})=\mathbf{G}(K_{n})
,
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}(K)=\mathbf{G}(K_{n})^{n\sigma}
, and
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}(\tilde{K})=\mathbf{G}(K_{n})^{\tilde{n}\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}}
where
\tilde{n}=\tau(\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}})
. (Recall that
\tilde{K}=k_{\dtame}^{\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}}
.) Since
{n}\in\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
, we have
\tilde{n}\in\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
. In fact, since
{{}^{n\sigma}\!}\tilde{n}=\tilde{n}
, we conclude that
\tilde{n}\in\Stab_{{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}(K)}(x_{0})
. From Remark
1.3.1
we have that
n
, and hence
\tilde{n}
, belongs to
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}(K_{n})={{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}(K_{n})
and hence
\tilde{n}\in{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}(K)
. From Lemma
2.3.5
we conclude that
\tilde{n}\in{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}(K)_{x_{0},0}\leq{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}(K)_{x_{0},0}
.

Since
x_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},K_{n})={\mathcal{B}}^{\red}({{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}},K_{n})
is fixed by both
\sigma
and
n
, it is fixed by
n\sigma
. Since
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}
is
K
-quasi-split, we can choose a maximally
K
-split
K
-torus
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
in
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}
and a Borel
K
-subgroup
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
of
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}
containing
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
such that
x_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}({{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime},K)=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}({{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime},K_{n})^{n\sigma}
and the topologically semisimple element
\tilde{n}\in{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{G}}(K)_{x_{0},0}
belongs to
{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}(K)
.

Since
n\sigma{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}={{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{n}\in{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}(K)
, we have
\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}=\tilde{n}^{-1}(n\sigma)^{\ell^{\prime}}{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}={{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
. Similarly, since
n\sigma{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}={{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{n}\in{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}(K)\leq{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}(K)
, we have
\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}=\tilde{n}^{-1}(n\sigma)^{\ell^{\prime}}{{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}={{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
.

Thus, there is a
\tilde{K}
-Borel-torus pair
(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(\tilde{K})={{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}(\tilde{K})
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}(\tilde{K})={{}_{\tau}\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}(\tilde{K})
. Note that
n\sigma(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
and
x_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}({{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime},K)\subset\mathcal{A}^{\prime}({{}_{\tau}\mathbf{A}}^{\prime},\tilde{K})=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\tilde{K})
.
∎

Corollary 3.2.3
.

If
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
is tame, then

n
is
\sigma
-conjugate by an element of
\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
to an element of
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{K}
is a tame extension of
K
.

Thanks to Lemma
3.2.1
we can find
a
\tilde{K}
-Borel-torus pair
(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{G}
such that
n\sigma(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
and
x_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\tilde{K})\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\tilde{K})
.
Choose
h\in\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
such that
h({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B})=(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
. Since
\sigma({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B})=({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B})
and
n\sigma(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})=(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
, we then have

h^{-1}n\sigma(h)({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B})=h^{-1}n\sigma h({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B})=h^{-1}n\sigma(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})=h^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B}^{\prime})=({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B}).

Thus
h^{-1}n\sigma(h)\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\cap\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}={\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.4
.

If
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
is tame, then

n
is
\sigma
-conjugate by an element of
\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
to an element of
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0^{+}}=A_{0}{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0^{+}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\tilde{K}
is a tame extension of
K
.

From Corollary
3.2.3
we know that there exists
h\in\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
such that
h^{-1}n\sigma(h)\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}
.

We identify
\mathsf{A}
in
\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{x_{0}}
by
\mathsf{A}\leq\mathsf{G}_{x_{0}}={\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{x_{0}}^{\bar{\sigma}}}\leq\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{x_{0}}
.
Note that
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
equals

C_{\tilde{\mathsf{G}}_{x_{0}}}(\mathsf{A})
and
\mathsf{A}
has finite index in
\Fix_{\bar{\sigma}}(\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
.

The map
x\mapsto\bar{\sigma}(x)
defines a finite order automorphism of
{\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}}
. Since
\mathsf{A}
has finite index in
\Fix_{\bar{\sigma}}(\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
and
\cohom^{1}(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathsf{A})\cong\Hom(\tilde{\Gamma},\mathsf{A})
is finite, we conclude that

\Fix_{\bar{\sigma}}({\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}}/\mathsf{A})
is finite. Thus, by
[
46
, Theorem 10.1]
, we conclude that the map
\bar{a}\mapsto\bar{\sigma}(\bar{a})^{-1}\bar{a}
from
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}/\mathsf{A}
to itself is surjective. Consequently, there exists
\bar{y}\in\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
such that, modulo
\mathsf{A}
,
\bar{\sigma}(\bar{y})^{-1}\bar{y}
is congruent to the image of
h^{-1}n\sigma(h)
in
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. Let
y
be an element of
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}
that lifts
\bar{y}
. Since
