Remark 3.6.7
.

In the tame case, the location of a point
x_{T_{n}}
in
\bar{C}^{\prime}
that occurs for a
K
-minisotropic maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
depends only on the existence of
\ell_{n}^{\text{th}}
roots of unity in
K
. Thus, in the tame setting the set of points that occur depends only on
\tilde{\Pi}
and the image of
\sigma
in the automorphism group of the Dynkin diagram associated to
\tilde{\Pi}
.
It seems likely that no additional points occur when we remove the tameness assumption, but I don’t know how to prove this. See also Remark
6.3.4
and the discussion immediately preceding Definition
9.0.2
.

3.7.
A comment on other vertices in
\bar{C}^{\prime}

In this subsection we assume that
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split; that is,
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}=\mathbf{A}
.
See Remark
3.7.3
for a discussion of the situation when
\mathbf{G}
is not
K
-split.

Suppose
y
is a vertex in
\bar{C}^{\prime}
. Let
\Phi(y)\leq\Phi
denote the root system of
\mathsf{G}_{y}
with respect to
\mathsf{A}
. Let
\mathbf{G}(y)
denote the connected reductive group generated by
\mathbf{A}={\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and the root groups
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}
where
\alpha
runs over those elements of
\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A})
belonging to
\Phi(y)
.
As affine spaces, we may and do identify the apartment of
A
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G(y))
with
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)
; under this identification, every
G(y)
-facet in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)
will be a union of
G
-facets.

Note that since
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split, we have that
y
is absolutely special in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G(y))
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(y)\leq G(y)_{y,0}
denote a Tits group for
\tilde{W}(y):=N_{\mathbf{G}(y)}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})/{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}=N_{\mathbf{G}(y)}(\mathbf{A})/\mathbf{A}
. Let
C(y)^{\prime}
denote the unique
G(y)
-alcove in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)
that contains
C^{\prime}
. The chamber
C(y)^{\prime}
determines a set of simple affine roots
\Delta(y)
for
\Psi(\mathbf{G}(y),\mathbf{A},K,\nu)
.

Suppose
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(y)
is tame and
\sigma
-elliptic. Let
\ell_{n}
denote the order of
n
and choose a uniformizer
\pi\in K_{n}=k_{\dtame}^{\sigma^{\ell_{n}}}
. Define a
\ell_{n}^{\text{th}}
root of unity by
\sigma(\pi)=\xi\pi
. Thanks to the work above with
y
in place of
x_{0}
, we can find
\lambda_{n}\in\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})
and
h\in G(y)_{y,0}
such that

•

h^{-1}n\sigma(h)=\lambda_{n}(\xi)
and

•

y_{n}=y+\lambda_{n}/\ell_{n}\in\overline{C(y)}^{\prime}
.

If we define
g_{n}=\lambda_{n}(\pi)h^{-1}\lambda_{n}(\pi)^{-1}\in\mathbf{G}(y)(K_{n})_{y_{n},0}
, then
n
and
g_{n}^{-1}\sigma(g_{n})\in N_{\mathbf{G}(y)(K_{n})}(\mathbf{A})
have the same image in
\tilde{W}(y)
. The maximal
K
-torus
{{}^{g_{n}}\!}\mathbf{A}={{}^{g_{n}}\!}{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
of
\mathbf{G}(y)
is
K
-minisotropic and the unique point in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G(y))
associated to it is
y_{n}
.

Example 3.7.1
.

We offer a cautionary example to show that the point identified in
\bar{C}^{\prime}
for a given
G
-conjugacy class of maximal

-minisotropic tori might not be the point identified in
\overline{C(y)}^{\prime}
for a corresponding
G(y)
-conjugacy class.
Adopt the notation of Example
3.6.3
. Suppose
y
is the vertex at the right angle in Figure
2
; i.e., the vertex there with the label
x_{-1}
. In Figure
5
we identify the unique point, denoted
y_{-1}
, in
\overline{C(y)}^{\prime}
corresponding to the Coxeter element
-1\in A_{1}\times\tilde{A}_{1}
in the Weyl group of
\mathbf{G}(y)\cong{\mathrm{SO}}_{4}
. The dotted lines show the
{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
-facet structure, and the shaded region is
C^{\prime}
. Note that

and

are

-conjugate.

Remark 3.7.2
.

We continue to assume that
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split. Observe that for
y\neq x_{0}
, the pinning
(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A}={\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B},\{X_{a}\}_{a\in\Pi})
of
\mathbf{G}
from Section
1.3
does not define a pinning of
\mathsf{G}_{y}
. However, if
\mathbf{B}(y)
denotes the Borel subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(y)
generated by
\mathbf{A}
and those root groups
\mathbf{U}_{\alpha}
where
\alpha
is the gradient of some
\psi\in\Delta(y)
with
\psi(y)=0
, then it is possible to choose a pinning
(\mathbf{G}(y),\mathbf{A}={\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B}(y),\{Y_{a}\}_{a\in\Pi(y)})
of
\mathbf{G}(y)
that is compatible, in the obvious sense, with
y
such that
Y_{b}=X_{b}
for all
b\in\Pi\cap\Pi(y)
. In fact, we can fix pinnings in such a way that if
y,y^{\prime}
are two vertices in
\bar{C}^{\prime}
with pinnings
(\mathbf{G}(y),\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}(y),\{Y_{a}\}_{a\in\Pi(y)})
and
(\mathbf{G}(y^{\prime}),\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}(y^{\prime}),\{Y^{\prime}_{a}\}_{a\in\Pi(y^{\prime})})
that are compatible with
y
and
y^{\prime}
respectively, then
Y_{b}=Y^{\prime}_{b}
for all
b\in\Pi(y)\cap\Pi(y^{\prime})
.

Remark 3.7.3
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is not
K
-split, then one needs to be more careful. For example, suppose
y
is a non-special vertex in the building
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G},K)
where
\mathbf{G}=\SU(4)
splits over a quadratic extension
E
of
K
. In this case
y
is special in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G},E)
, and there does not exist a full rank reductive subgroup
\mathbf{H}
of
\mathbf{G}
such that
y
is absolutely special in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{H},K)
and
\mathsf{G}_{y}=\mathsf{H}_{y}
. In general, one might hope to choose a subgroup
\mathbf{H}
of
\mathbf{G}
such that (a) the
K
-rank of
\mathbf{H}
is the same as the
K
-rank of
\mathbf{G}
, (b)
y
is absolutely special in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{H},K)
, and (c) we have
\mathsf{G}_{y}=\mathsf{H}_{y}
; this is being investigated in
[
37
]
.

4.
On the existence of
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori of
\mathbf{G}

From now until the end of the paper we assume that
k
is a nonarchimedean local field; in particular,
{\mathfrak{f}}
is finite. Recall that
{\Fro}
is an element of
\Gal(\bar{k}/k)
that lifts a topological generator of
\Gal(K/k)
.

In this section we provide, in Lemma
4.0.3
, a criterion for determining when the
G
-orbit of a
K
-minisotropic maximal
K
-torus of
\mathbf{G}
contains a torus defined over
k
.
Recall that
\mathcal{C}
denotes the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
.

Lemma 4.0.1
.

If
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{C}
, then
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})\in\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{C}
. Choose a strongly regular semisimple
\gamma\in T
. Note that
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})=C_{\mathbf{G}}({\Fro}(\gamma))
is again a maximal
K
-torus of
\mathbf{G}
. Since
{\Fro}(G)=G
, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 4.0.2
.

If
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
, then the torus
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
is also a
K
-minisotropic maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
.

Proof.

For all
\delta\in\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
we have
\delta({\Fro}(\mathbf{T}))={\Fro}(\delta^{\prime}(\mathbf{T}))={\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
where
\delta^{\prime}={\Fro}^{-1}\delta{\Fro}\in\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
. Thus,
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
is a maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
. An element
\chi\in\X^{*}(\mathbf{T})
is fixed by
\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
if and only if
{\Fro}(\chi)\in\X^{*}({\Fro}(\mathbf{T}))
is fixed by
\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
; so
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
is also a
K
-minisotropic torus.
∎

Lemma 4.0.3
.

Assume that
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
, not necessarily defined over
k
.
Let
\mathcal{O}
denote the
G
-orbit of
\mathbf{T}
. There exists a torus in
\mathcal{O}
that is
{\Fro}
-stable if and only if
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

If there is a torus in
\mathcal{O}
that is
{\Fro}
-stable, then without loss of generality we may assume
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})=\mathbf{T}
. This immediately implies
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}
.

Suppose
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}
.
Without loss of generality the point
x_{T}
corresponding to
\mathbf{T}
belongs to
\bar{C}^{\prime}
.
Since
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}
, there exists
h\in G
such that
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})={{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}
. Since
\bar{C}^{\prime}
is
{\Fro}
-stable, we have
{\Fro}(x_{T})\in\bar{C}^{\prime}
. On the other hand, by uniqueness we have
h\cdot x_{T}={\Fro}(x_{T})
. Thanks to Lemma
2.2.2
we conclude that
h\cdot x_{T}=x_{T}
and so
h\in\Stab_{G}(x_{T})
. From Lemma
2.3.1
we know
\Stab_{G}(x_{T})=G_{x_{T},0}T
, so without loss of generality we may assume
h\in G_{x_{T},0}
. From Lang-Steinberg, there exists
k\in G_{x_{T},0}
for which
h^{-1}=k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)
. So
{\Fro}(T)={{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}={{}^{{\Fro}(k^{-1})k}\!}\mathbf{T}
, which implies
{\Fro}({{}^{k}\!}\mathbf{T})={{}^{k}\!}\mathbf{T}
.
∎

Corollary 4.0.4
.

Suppose
\gamma\in G
is
K
-elliptic and strongly regular semisimple. If the
G
-orbit of
\gamma
is
{\Fro}
-stable, then the
G
-orbit of
\gamma
contains a Frobenius-fixed point.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{T}
denote the centralizer of
\gamma
. Since
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
is the centralizer of
{\Fro}(\gamma)
and
{\Fro}(\gamma)
is
G
-conjugate to
\gamma
, we conclude that the
G
-orbit of
\mathbf{T}
is
{\Fro}
-stable. Thanks to Lemma
4.0.3
there exists
h\in G
such that
{{}^{h}\!}T
is Frobenius fixed. Replacing
\gamma
with
{{}^{h}\!}\gamma
we may and do assume that
\gamma\in T
and
\mathbf{T}
is defined over
k
. Note that
{\Fro}(\gamma)\in T
and
{\Fro}(\gamma)={{}^{n}\!}\gamma
for some
n\in G
. Since
\gamma
is strongly regular semisimple, we must have
n\in N_{G}(T)\leq\Stab_{G}(x_{T})
. From Lemma
2.3.1
we know
\Stab_{G}(x_{T})=G_{x_{T},0}T
, so without loss of generality we may assume
n\in G_{x_{T},0}
. From Lang-Steinberg, there exists
j\in G_{x_{T},0}
for which
n^{-1}=j^{-1}{\Fro}(j)
. The element
{{}^{j}\!}\gamma
is Frobenius fixed.
∎

Remark 4.0.5
.

When the derived group of
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected, Corollary
4.0.4
may be derived from
[
28
, Theorem 4.1 and Lemma 3.3]
.

5.
On the existence of
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori of
\mathbf{G}
in the tame situation

Note that
{\Fro}^{-1}\sigma{\Fro}=\sigma^{q}
. Moreover,
{\Fro}
acts on the set of tame extensions of
K
as well as
\tilde{W}
and
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
.

5.1.
A simple criterion

In this section we translate, in the tame setting, the criterion of Lemma
4.0.3
into a condition on the
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in the Weyl group.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

If
\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
, then
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})\in\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{O}\in\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
. Let
E
be a tame extension of
K
over which
\mathbf{T}
splits.
Thanks to Lemma
4.0.1
we know that
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
is again a maximal
K
-torus of
\mathbf{G}
. Since
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
splits over the tame extension
{\Fro}(E)
of
K
, we have
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})\in\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
.
∎

Since
\sigma^{q}
is a generator for
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
, it also stabilizes every tame extension of
K
; in particular, it stabilizes
\tilde{K}
.
Let
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim_{\sigma^{q}}}
denote the set of
\sigma^{q}
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}
and let
\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}\colon\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim_{\sigma^{q}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
denote the resulting map from the set of
\sigma^{q}
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}
to the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
.

For
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
w\in\tilde{W}
set

N_{d}(w)=w\cdot\sigma(w)\cdots\sigma^{(d-2)}(w)\cdot\sigma^{(d-1)}(w).

The map
N_{q}
induces a well-defined map from
W_{\sim_{\sigma}}
to
W_{\sim_{\sigma^{q}}}
and
{\Fro}
defines a map from
W_{\sim_{\sigma^{q}}}
to
W_{\sim_{\sigma}}
.

Lemma 5.1.2
.

The diagram below commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim_{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim_{\sigma^{q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim_{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}

Moreover, each map is a bijection.

Proof.

The statement (and proof) of Lemma
3.4.1
holds when
\sigma
is replaced by any generator of
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
, so we conclude that all three vertical arrows are bijections.

Suppose
c
is a
\sigma
-conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}
.
Then there exists
g\in\mathbf{G}(k_{\dtame})
such that the image of
g^{-1}\sigma(g)
in
\tilde{W}
is an element of
c
. We have
(\Id\circ\varphi_{\sigma})(c)=\{{{}^{hg}\!}{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\,|\,h\in G\}
. The image of

g^{-1}\sigma^{q}(g)=(g^{-1}\sigma(g))\sigma(g^{-1}\sigma(g))\cdots\sigma^{q-1}(g^{-1}\sigma(g))

in
\tilde{W}
belongs to
N_{q}(c)
and so

(\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}\circ N_{q})(c)=\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}(N_{q}(c))=\{{{}^{hg}\!}{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\,|\,h\in G\}.

Thus
\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}\circ N_{q}=\Id\circ\varphi_{\sigma}
.

Suppose
\tilde{c}
is a
\sigma^{q}
-conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}=\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}(\tilde{c})\in\mathcal{C}
. There exists
\tilde{g}\in\mathbf{G}(k_{\dtame})
such that the image of
\tilde{g}^{-1}\sigma^{q}(\tilde{g})
in
\tilde{W}
is an element of
\tilde{c}
. We have
({\Fro}\circ\varphi_{\sigma^{q}})(\tilde{c})={\Fro}(\tilde{\mathcal{O}})=\{{{}^{h{\Fro}(\tilde{g})}\!}{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\,|\,h\in G\}
.
The image of

{\Fro}(\tilde{g}^{-1}\sigma^{q}(\tilde{g}))={\Fro}(\tilde{g})^{-1}\sigma({\Fro}(\tilde{g}))
in
\tilde{W}
belongs to
{\Fro}(\tilde{c})
and so

(\varphi_{\sigma}\circ{\Fro})(\tilde{c})=\varphi_{\sigma}({\Fro}(\tilde{c}))=\{{{}^{h{\Fro}(\tilde{g})}\!}{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\,|\,h\in G\}.

Since this is
{\Fro}(\tilde{\mathcal{O}})
, we have
\varphi_{\sigma}\circ{\Fro}={\Fro}\circ\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}
.

Since
G
is
{\Fro}
-stable, the map
{\Fro}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
is the inverse to
{\Fro}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
. The result follows.
∎

Corollary 5.1.3
.

Suppose
c\in\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim_{\sigma}}
is
\sigma
-elliptic.
There exists a torus in
\varphi_{\sigma}(c)
that is
{\Fro}
-stable if and only if
c=({\Fro}\circ N_{q})(c)
.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.1.2
we know that
{\Fro}(\varphi_{\sigma}(c))=\varphi_{\sigma}(c)
if and only if
c=({\Fro}\circ N_{q})(c)
. The result follows from Lemmas
3.3.6
and
4.0.3
.
∎

5.2.
An application of the rationality of Weyl groups

A finite group
H
is said to be
rational
provided that for any
h\in H
we have that
h^{j}
is
H
-conjugate to
h
whenever
j
is relatively prime to the order of
h
. There are many equivalent definitions of rationality, and the nomenclature is explained by the fact
[
43
, Section 13.1]
that
H
is rational if and only if every irreducible character of
H
takes values in
\mathbb{Q}
.

It is known that every Weyl group is a rational group (see, for example,
[
15
, Lemma 2.1.2]
,
[
26
, Section 3 of Chapter 2 and Chapter 5]
, or
[
45
, Theorem 8.5]
).

Corollary 5.2.1
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split, then
the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}

commutes and each map is a bijection. Here
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim}
is the set of
\tilde{W}
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}
. Moreover, the vertical maps are independent of the choice of topological generator
\sigma
of
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
. When
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-split, all maps are independent of the choice of topological generators
{\Fro}
for
\Gal(K/k)
and
\sigma
for
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split, we have
\mathbf{A}={\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and
\sigma
acts trivially on
\tilde{W}
. Thus,
N_{q}(\dot{w})=\dot{w}^{q}
for all
\dot{w}\in\tilde{W}
.

Choose
w\in\tilde{W}^{\dtame}
.
Since
\tilde{W}
is a rational group and
q
is relatively prime to the order of
w
, we have that
w^{q}
and
w
are conjugate in
\tilde{W}
. Thus
N_{q}(c)=c
for all
c\in\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim_{\sigma}}
.
The claims about the commutativity of the diagram and bijectivity of its maps follow from Lemma
5.1.2
.

We now show that the vertical maps are independent of the choice of the topological generator
\sigma
. Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be another choice of topological generator for
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
. Suppose
w\in c\in\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim}
. Choose
g\in\mathbf{G}(k_{\dtame})
such that
g^{-1}\sigma(g)\in N_{\mathbf{G}(k_{\dtame})}(\mathbf{A})
has image
w
in
\tilde{W}
. Let
E/K
be the splitting field of
{{}^{g}\!}\mathbf{A}
. Set
\ell=[E:K]
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
g\in\mathbf{G}(E)
. Note that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, the elements
(g^{-1}\sigma(g))^{k}
and
g^{-1}\sigma^{k}(g)
of
N_{\mathbf{G}(E)}(\mathbf{A})
have the same image in
\tilde{W}
, namely
w^{k}
. Putting
k=\ell
, we conclude that
w^{\ell}=1
. Let
\bar{\sigma}
(resp.
\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}
) denote the image of
\sigma
(resp.
\sigma^{\prime}
) in
\Gal(E/K)
. Since
\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}
generates
\Gal(E/K)
, there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
with
(m,\ell)=1
such that
\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}=\bar{\sigma}^{m}
. Putting
k=m
, we conclude that the image
g^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}(g)\in N_{\mathbf{G}(E)}(\mathbf{A})
is
w^{m}
. Since
m
is relatively prime to the order of
w
and
\tilde{W}
is a rational group,
w
and
w^{m}
are conjugate in
\tilde{W}
. Thus
w^{m}\in c
. We conclude that
\varphi_{\sigma}(c)=\varphi_{\sigma^{\prime}}(c)={{}^{G}\!}({{}^{g}\!}\mathbf{A})
.

Suppose now that
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-split. In this case any choice of
{\Fro}
acts trivially on
\tilde{W}
; hence the horizontal maps are both the identity. Hence, when
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-split, the maps in the diagram are independent of the choice of both
{\Fro}
and
\sigma
.
∎

Remark 5.2.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{G}
is not
K
-split and
\sigma^{\prime}
is another choice of topological generator for
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
. If the image of
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
in
\Aut(\tilde{\Pi})
agree, then by arguing as in the proof of Corollary
5.2.1
it follows from
[
1
, Proposition 2.6]
that
\varphi_{\sigma}
and

\varphi_{\sigma^{\prime}}
induce the same map from
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim}
to
\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
.

Remark 5.2.3
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-split, then from Corollary
5.2.1
the condition
c=({\Fro}\circ N_{q})(c)
of Corollary
5.1.3
holds for every tame elliptic conjugacy class
c
of the Weyl group. However, if we remove the assumption that
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-split, then this condition is no longer guaranteed to hold. For example, suppose
k^{\prime}
is an unramified degree three extension of
k
and let
\mathbf{G}=R_{k^{\prime}/k}{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
. As a
K
-group, we have
\mathbf{G}_{K}\cong{\mathrm{G}_{2}}\times{\mathrm{G}_{2}}\times{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
, and
{\Fro}
-acts by cyclically permuting the three copies of
{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
. By placing one of each of the three distinct elliptic conjugacy classes
A_{1}\times\tilde{A}_{1}
,
A_{2}
, and
{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
on each of the three
{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
factors of
\mathbf{G}
, we obtain an elliptic conjugacy class in the Weyl group of
R_{k^{\prime}/k}{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
which is not
{\Fro}\circ N_{q}
-stable.

5.3.
Cautionary example

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
. We know from our work above that if
\mathbf{T}
is
K
-minisotropic and
{\Fro}({{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T})={{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T}
, then
{{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T}
contains a
k
-torus. At the other extreme, we also know that if
\mathbf{T}
is a maximally
K
-split maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
, then
{{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T}
is
{\Fro}
-stable and, since every maximal
k
-split torus of
\mathbf{G}
is contained in a maximally
K
-split maximal
k
-torus of
\mathbf{G}
,
{{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T}
contains a
k
-torus.

These examples and the results above suggest that there might be an elegant general theory relating the existence of
k
-tori in an orbit
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
to the
{\Fro}
-invariance of
\mathcal{O}
. In this section we present an example showing that the natural condition
{\Fro}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}
does not guarantee that
\mathcal{O}
contains a
k
-torus.

Example 5.3.1
.

Suppose that the residual characteristic of
k
is larger than three and let
\mathbf{H}
be a connected reductive group of type
A_{2}
such that
H^{{\Fro}}\cong\SL_{1}(D)
where
D
is a division algebra of index
3
over
k
. Recall that we may identify
H=\mathbf{H}(K)
with
\SL_{3}(K)
. Let
\mathbf{A}
denote a maximal
K
-split
k
-torus in
\mathbf{H}
; it is unique up to
H^{\Fro}
-conjugacy. Let
\tilde{W}=N_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathbf{A})/\mathbf{A}
. Suppose
C
is the alcove in
\mathcal{A}(A)\subset{\mathcal{B}}(H)
for which
C^{{\Fro}}\neq\emptyset
. Let
\{\psi_{0},\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}
be the simple affine
K
-roots determined by
\mathbf{H}
,
\mathbf{A}
,
\nu
, and
C
. We assume that the
\psi_{i}
are labeled such that
{\Fro}(\psi_{i})=\psi_{i+1}
mod
3
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
denote a maximal
K
-torus in
\mathbf{H}
that corresponds, under Corollary
3.4.3
, to the
\tilde{W}
-conjugacy class of the simple reflection
w_{\dot{\psi}_{1}}
. Since this
\tilde{W}
-conjugacy class is
{\Fro}
-stable, from Corollary
5.2.1
we conclude that
{\Fro}(\mathbf{T})
is
H
-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}
. Since
\mathbf{T}
splits over a quadratic extension of
K
and all maximal
k
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
correspond to extensions
E\leq D
whose ramification degree over
k
is not
2
, we conclude that
{{}^{H}\!}\mathbf{T}
cannot contain a
k
-torus.

5.4.
Results when
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame

We gather here the statements of the main results of this section under the hypothesis that
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame. Recall that when
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame we have
\tilde{W}=\tilde{W}^{\dtame}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\dtame}
(see Definition
3.4.2
and Corollary
3.4.3
). The following three results follow immediately from Lemma
5.1.2
, Corollary
5.1.3
, and Corollary
5.2.1
.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame. The diagram below commutes.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}_{\sim_{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}_{\sim_{\sigma^{q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}_{\sim_{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma^{q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}

Moreover, each map is a bijection. ∎

Corollary 5.4.2
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame. Suppose
c\in\tilde{W}_{\sim_{\sigma}}
is
\sigma
-elliptic.
There exists a torus in
\varphi_{\sigma}(c)
that is
{\Fro}
-stable if and only if
c=({\Fro}\circ N_{q})(c)
. ∎

Corollary 5.4.3
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame and
K
-split, then
the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}_{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{W}_{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Fro}}

commutes and each map is a bijection. Here
\tilde{W}_{\sim}
is the set of
\tilde{W}
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
. When
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame and
k
-split, all maps are independent of the choice of generators
{\Fro}
for
\Gal(K/k)
and
\sigma
for
\Gal(k_{\dtame}/K)
. ∎

Corollary 5.4.4
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame and
k
-split, then every
G
-orbit of
K
-minisotropic maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
contains a torus defined over
k
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-split, the top horizontal map of the diagram in Corollary
5.4.3
becomes the identity. The result follows from Lemma
4.0.3
.
∎

6.
Results about
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori in
\mathbf{G}

Suppose that
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus of
\mathbf{G}
.
The set of
{\Fro}
-stable tori in the
G
-orbit of
\mathbf{T}
is denoted
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
. In this section we parameterize three sets related to
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
: we parameterize the
G^{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
; we parameterize the
k
-stable-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
(see Definition
6.4.1
); and we parameterize, up to
G^{\Fro}
-conjugacy, the
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
(see Definition
6.2.1
).

Recall from §
2.2
that we can associate a
point
x_{T}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
to
T
.
Since
\mathbf{T}
is defined over
k
, we have
{\Fro}(T)=T
and so by uniqueness we conclude that
x_{T}
is Frobenius fixed. Thus, we can assume
x_{T}\in\bar{C}^{\prime{\Fro}}\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)^{\Fro}={\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},k)
. Recall that
F
is the unique facet in
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
whose image in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
contains
x_{T}
.

6.1.
Points in the reduced building associated to
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori of
\mathbf{G}

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus of
\mathbf{G}
.

Lemma 6.1.1
.

The set

G^{{\Fro}}\cdot x_{T}\cap\bar{C}^{\prime}{}^{{\Fro}}

has cardinality one.

Remark 6.1.2
.

The proof below also works if
k
is not residually quasi-split, but we must replace the alcove
C^{\prime{\Fro}}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},k)
with an alcove
D
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},k)
that is contained in the closure of
{C}^{\prime}
. The statement would then read:

\mathbf{G}(k)\cdot x_{T}\cap\bar{D}\,\text{
has cardinality one.}

Proof.

From Lemma
2.2.2
we know that

G\cdot x_{T}\cap\bar{C}^{\prime}
has cardinality one, and so
G^{{\Fro}}\cdot x_{T}\cap\bar{C}^{\prime}{}^{{\Fro}}
has at most one point. Since
x_{T}\in\bar{C}^{\prime}{}^{{\Fro}}
, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 6.1.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is a maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
. If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is
G
-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}
, then there exist
h\in G^{{\Fro}}
and
k^{\prime}\in\Stab_{G}(x_{T})
such that
{{}^{k^{\prime}h}\!}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is
G
-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}
, we have that
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
. Thus, we may associate to
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
a point
x_{T^{\prime}}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},k)
. Choose
h\in G^{{\Fro}}
such that
hx_{T^{\prime}}\in\bar{C}^{\prime{\Fro}}
.

From Lemma
2.2.2
we know
hx_{T^{\prime}}=x_{T}
. Since
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
are
G
-conjugate, there exists
k^{\prime}\in G
such that
\mathbf{T}={{}^{k^{\prime}h}\!}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
. So
x_{T}=k^{\prime}hx_{T^{\prime}}=k^{\prime}x_{T}
, which implies
k^{\prime}\in\Stab_{G}(x_{T})
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.4
.

Recall that in this section
x_{T}
is Frobenius fixed.

G\cdot x_{T}\cap{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)^{{\Fro}}=G^{{\Fro}}\cdot x_{T}

Proof.

It is enough to show that
G\cdot x_{T}\cap{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)^{{\Fro}}\subset G^{{\Fro}}\cdot x_{T}
. Suppose
g\in G
such that
g\cdot x_{T}
is Frobenius fixed. Then
g\cdot x_{T}
belongs to some alcove of
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)^{{\Fro}}
and so there exists
h\in G^{{\Fro}}
such that
h^{-1}g\cdot x_{T}\in\bar{C}^{\prime{\Fro}}
. From Lemma
2.2.2
the point
h^{-1}g\cdot x_{T}
must be
x_{T}
and so
{g}\cdot x_{T}=h\cdot x_{T}\in G^{{\Fro}}\cdot x_{T}
.
∎

6.2.
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}

For many questions in harmonic analysis we want to understand the ways to
k
-embed
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
up to
G^{\Fro}
-conjugacy.

Definition 6.2.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{S}
is a maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
. A
k
-embedding
of
\mathbf{S}
into
\mathbf{G}
is a map
f\colon\mathbf{S}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
such that

(1)

there exists
g\in G
such that
f(s)={{{}^{g}\!}s}
for all
s\in S
and

(2)

f
is a
k
-morphism.

Remark 6.2.2
.

In Definition
6.2.1
it would be more standard to take
g\in\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
. However, since
f
is completely determined by what it does to any strongly regular semisimple element of
T^{\Fro}
, from Lemma
2.1.1
we can restrict our attention to
g\in G
.

If
f\colon\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
is a
k
-embedding, then
f
is completely determined by where it sends any given strongly regular semisimple element of
T^{\Fro}
. So, we begin by studying strongly regular semisimple elements.

Definition 6.2.3
.

Suppose
\gamma\in G^{{\Fro}}
is strongly regular semisimple. An element
\gamma^{\prime}\in G^{\Fro}
is
k
-stably-conjugate
to
\gamma
provided that there exists
g\in G
such that
{{}^{g}\!}\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
denote the set of elements of
G^{{\Fro}}
that are
k
-stably-conjugate to
\gamma
.

Definition 6.2.4
.

T_{F}:=T\cap G_{F,0}

In the notation of Corollary
2.3.4
we have that
T_{F}=\eta[T_{\scon}]T_{0}
.
In general,
T_{F}
is neither
T
nor is it the parahoric subgroup
T_{0}
. For example, for
p>2
and a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus in
\SL_{2}
that splits over a quadratic ramified extension, we have
T/T_{F}
is trivial and
T_{F}/T_{0}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. However, for
p>2
and a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus in
\PGL_{2}
that splits over a quadratic ramified extension, we have
T/T_{F}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
and
T_{F}/T_{0}
is trivial.

Definition 6.2.5
.

For a
{\Fro}
-module
A
, let
A_{{\Fro}}
denote the
{\Fro}
-coinvariants. That is

A_{\Fro}=A/(1-{\Fro})A.

Thanks to Lang-Steinberg, we have
T_{0}\leq(1-{\Fro})T_{F}\leq T_{F}
. We can have
T_{0}\neq(1-{\Fro})T_{F}
and
(1-{\Fro})T_{F}\neq T_{F}
; to see this suppose
p>3
and consider ramified elliptic maximal
k
-tori in
\SL_{3}
for various choices of
k
.

Definition 6.2.6
.

Let
\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}:=T_{F}/T_{0}
.

Since
(1-{\Fro})T_{0}=T_{0}
, we have
\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}\cong(T_{F})_{{\Fro}}
. We will often use this identification.

Lemma 6.2.7
.

Suppose
\gamma\in T^{{\Fro}}
is strongly regular semisimple. There is natural bijection between the set of
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
and the Frobenius coinvariants of
T_{F}
.
That is

\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}/\text{$G^{{\Fro}}$-conjugacy}\,\,\longleftrightarrow\,\,(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}.

Remark 6.2.8
.

Lemma
6.2.7
implies that
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}
is a finite group. This also follows from the fact that
\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}^{\circ}=(1-{\Fro})\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}^{\circ}
.

Proof.

If
\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
, then there is an
h\in G
such that
\gamma^{\prime}={{}^{h}\!}\gamma
. Since
\mathbf{T}
is
k
-stably-conjugate to
C_{\mathbf{G}}(\gamma^{\prime})
, thanks to Lemma
6.1.3
there is a
g\in G^{{\Fro}}
such that the point in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
attached to both
\mathbf{T}
and
{{}^{g}\!}(C_{\mathbf{G}}(\gamma^{\prime}))
is
x_{T}
. That is, we may replace
\gamma^{\prime}
by
{{}^{gh}\!}\gamma
and assume, thanks to Lemma
2.3.1
, that
\gamma^{\prime}={{}^{k}\!}\gamma
for
k\in G_{F,0}
. Since
{{}^{k}\!}\gamma={\Fro}({{}^{k}\!}\gamma)={{}^{{\Fro}(k)}\!}\gamma
, we have
k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)\in T_{F}
. Moreover, if
\gamma^{\prime}={{}^{k^{\prime}}\!}\gamma
for
k^{\prime}\in G_{F,0}
, then
k^{-1}k^{\prime}\in T_{F}
which implies that
k^{\prime}=ks
for some
s\in T_{F}
. Hence
(k^{\prime})^{-1}{\Fro}(k^{\prime})=s^{-1}(k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)){\Fro}(s)=(k^{-1}{\Fro}(k))(s^{-1}{\Fro}(s))
. That is, we have a well-defined map
\lambda\colon\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}\rightarrow T_{F}/(1-{\Fro})T_{F}=(T_{F})_{{\Fro}}
.

On the other hand, if
t\in T_{F}
, then, thanks to Lang-Steinberg, there is a
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)=t
. Note that
{{}^{k}\!}\gamma\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
and
\lambda({{}^{k}\!}\gamma)
is the image of
t
in
T_{F}/(1-{\Fro})T_{F}
.

To complete the proof, we need to show that if
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
, then
\gamma_{1}
is
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugate to
\gamma_{2}
if and only if
\lambda(\gamma_{1})=\lambda(\gamma_{2})
. As above, after possibly conjugating by an element of
G^{{\Fro}}
, we can write
\gamma_{i}={{}^{k_{i}}\!}\gamma
with
k_{i}\in G_{F,0}
.

Suppose
\lambda(\gamma_{1})=\lambda(\gamma_{2})
. Then there exists
s\in T_{F}
such that

(k_{1}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{1}))(s^{-1}{\Fro}(s))=k_{2}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{2})
.
Note that
\gamma_{1}={{}^{k_{1}sk_{2}^{-1}}\!}\gamma_{2}
and
{\Fro}(k_{1}sk_{2}^{-1})=k_{1}sk_{2}^{-1}
, so
\gamma_{1}
is
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugate to
\gamma_{2}
.

Suppose
\gamma_{1}
is
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugate to
\gamma_{2}
. Then, thanks to Lemma
6.1.3
, there exists
h\in G^{{\Fro}}_{F,0}
such that
{{}^{h}\!}\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
. Note that
k_{2}^{-1}hk_{1}\in T_{F}
. Thus, there is a
t\in T_{F}
such that
k_{2}^{-1}hk_{1}=t^{-1}
, or
k_{2}=hk_{1}t
. Note that

k_{2}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{2})=t^{-1}k_{1}^{-1}h^{-1}{\Fro}(h){\Fro}(k_{1}){\Fro}(t)=(k_{1}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{1}))(t^{-1}{\Fro}(t)).

Hence,
\lambda(\gamma_{1})=\lambda(\gamma_{2})
.
∎

Corollary 6.2.9
.

Suppose
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{d}\in T_{F}
represent the elements of
(T_{F})_{{\Fro}}
and choose
k_{i}\in G_{F,0}
such that
k_{i}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{i})=t_{i}
. Then

\{f_{i}\colon\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}\,|\,\text{ $f_{i}(t)={{}^{k_{i}}\!}t$ for $t\in\mathbf{T}$ }\}

is a complete set of representatives for the
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
.

Proof.

If
\gamma\in T^{{\Fro}}
is strongly regular semisimple, then the
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the
G^{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
.
∎

6.3.
K
-minisotropic maximal tori in
\mathbf{G}
and the
\Omega
group

The original aim of this subsection was to prove Corollary
6.3.10
, which is used in the proof of Lemma
6.4.7
. The derivation of Corollary
6.3.10
gave rise to some interesting results about the
\Omega
group. We develop some of these results here.

Recall that
C
is a
{\Fro}
-stable alcove in
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
such that
F\subset\overline{C}
and
x_{T}
belongs to the image of
F
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
.
In this section we show that
T/T_{F}
is isomorphic to
\Omega=\Stab_{G}(C)/G_{C,0}
and that
\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(F)=T^{{\Fro}}G_{F,0}^{{\Fro}}
.

We begin by recalling a known fact, though I am not sure where it is proved in the literature.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

Suppose
H
is a facet in
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
with
H\subset\overline{C}
. There is a natural group homomorphism

\varphi\colon\Stab_{G}(H)\rightarrow\Omega

with kernel
G_{H,0}
. Moreover, if
H
is
{\Fro}
-stable, then
\varphi
is
{\Fro}
-equivariant and
\varphi
descends to a group homomorphism

\varphi\colon\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(H)\rightarrow\Omega^{{\Fro}},

which has kernel
G^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}
.

Proof.

Suppose
t\in\Stab_{G}(H)
. Since
H\subset\bar{C}
,
t\cdot H=H
, and all Borel subgroups in a connected reductive group are conjugate, there exists
k\in G_{H,0}
such that
kt\cdot C=C
; that is,
kt\in\Stab_{G}(C)
. Fix such a
k
. If
k^{\prime}\in G_{H,0}
also has the property that
k^{\prime}t\cdot C=C
, then

(k^{\prime}t)(kt)^{-1}=k^{\prime}k^{-1}\in G_{H,0}

and

k^{\prime}k^{-1}\cdot C=(k^{\prime}t)(kt)^{-1}\cdot C=C.

Since parabolic subgroups of a reductive group are self-normalizing (see also
[
14
, Lemma 4.2.1]
), we have
k^{\prime}k^{-1}\in G_{C,0}
. So,
(k^{\prime}t)(kt)^{-1}\in G_{C,0}
. Thus, as
G_{C,0}
is normal in
\Stab_{G}(C)
, we have
k^{\prime}t\in G_{C,0}kt=ktG_{C,0}
. Consequently, we have a function
\varphi\colon\Stab_{G}(H)\rightarrow\Stab_{G}(C)/G_{C,0}
defined by
t\mapsto ktG_{C,0}
.

To see that
\varphi
is a group homomorphism, fix
t,t^{\prime}\in\Stab_{G}(H)
and choose
k,k^{\prime}\in G_{H,0}
such that
\varphi(t)=ktG_{C,0}
and
\varphi(t^{\prime})=k^{\prime}t^{\prime}G_{C,0}
. A calculation shows that
\varphi(t)\varphi(t^{\prime})=(k{{}^{t}\!}k^{\prime})(tt^{\prime})G_{C,0}
. Since
(k{{}^{t}\!}k^{\prime})(tt^{\prime})C=C
, with
k{{}^{t}\!}k^{\prime}\in G_{H,0}
, we conclude that
\varphi(tt^{\prime})=\varphi(t)\varphi(t^{\prime})
.

By construction, the group homomorphism
\varphi
has kernel
G_{H,0}
.

Suppose now that
H
is
{\Fro}
-stable. We have
{\Fro}(\varphi(t))={\Fro}(k){\Fro}(t){\Fro}(G_{C,0})
. Since
H
and
C
are Frobenius stable, we have
{\Fro}(k)\in G_{H,0}
and
{\Fro}(G_{C,0})=G_{C,0}
. It follows that
{\Fro}(\varphi(t))={\Fro}(k){\Fro}(t)G_{C,0}=\varphi({\Fro}(t))
.

That
\varphi
descends to a map on Frobenius-fixed points

\varphi\colon\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(H)\rightarrow\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(C)/G^{{\Fro}}_{C,0}

with kernel
G_{H,0}^{\Fro}
follows by repeating the initial part of this argument with all the groups replaced by their Frobenius-fixed points.
∎

Lemma 6.3.2
.

There is a natural surjective,
{\Fro}
-equivariant, group homomorphism from
T
to
\Omega
with kernel

T_{F}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
6.3.1
there is a
{\Fro}
-equivariant map
\varphi\colon\Stab_{G}(F)\rightarrow\Omega
with kernel
G_{F,0}
. Since
T\leq\Stab_{G}(F)
and
T
is
{\Fro}
-stable, we may restrict
\varphi
to
T
to obtain a
{\Fro}
-equivariant group homomorphism
\varphi\colon T\rightarrow\Omega
.

We first show that
T_{F}=\ker(\varphi)
. If
t\in T_{F}
, then
t^{-1}\in G_{F,0}
and
t^{-1}t\cdot C=C
. So,
\varphi(t)=t^{-1}tG_{C,0}=G_{C,0}
, and we conclude that
t\in\ker(\varphi)
. On the other hand, if
t\in\ker(\varphi)
, then there exists
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
kt\cdot G_{C,0}=G_{C,0}
. This means
tG_{C,0}=k^{-1}G_{C,0}\subset G_{F,0}
. We conclude that
t\in G_{F,0}
, hence
t
is in
T_{F}
.

We now show that
\varphi
is surjective. Suppose
\omega\in\Omega
and choose
g\in\Stab_{G}(C)
such that
\omega=gG_{C,0}
. From Lemma
2.2.2
we conclude that
gF=F
. Hence, from Lemma
2.3.1
there exist
t\in T
and
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
g=kt
. We conclude that
\varphi(t)=\omega
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.3
.

The point
x_{T}
is fixed by
\Omega
.

Proof.

Suppose
g\in\Stab_{G}(C)
. From Lemma
6.3.2
, there exist
t\in T
and
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
tk=g
. Since
kx_{T}=x_{T}
and
tx_{T}=x_{T}
, we conclude that
gx_{T}=x_{T}
.
∎

Remark 6.3.4
.

Since the point
x_{T}
is independent of isogeny type (see Remark
2.2.1
), we see that
x_{T}
must be invariant under the action of
\Omega_{\ad}=\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(C)/(G_{\ad})_{C,0}
. So, for example, for groups of type
A_{n}
the point
x_{T}
must be the barycenter of
C^{\prime}
.

Remark 6.3.5
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is almost simple, then
{\Fro}(x_{T})=x_{T}
. In the tame case, this follows from
[
1
, Proposition 6.8]
. To see that this holds in general, express
x_{T}
in barycentric coordinates:
x_{T}=\sum_{\psi\in\Delta}{x_{\psi}}v_{\psi}
where the
v_{\psi}
are the vertices of
\bar{C}
,
x_{\psi}\geq 0
, and
\sum x_{\psi}=1
. As noted in Remark
6.3.4
, we have
\omega(x_{T})=x_{T}
for all
\omega\in\Omega_{\ad}
. Thus,
x_{\psi}=x_{\omega(\psi)}
for all
\omega\in\Omega_{\ad}
and
\psi\in\Delta
. By case-by-case checking, one sees that this implies that
x_{\psi}=x_{\tau(\psi)}
for all automorphisms
\tau
of the affine Dynkin diagram. Since
{\Fro}(C)=C
, we conclude that
{\Fro}(\Delta)=\Delta
, and hence
{\Fro}
induces an automorphism of the affine Dynkin diagram. Thus,
x_{{\Fro}(\psi)}=x_{\psi}
for all
\psi\in\Delta
, and so
{\Fro}(x_{T})=x_{T}
. If
\mathbf{G}
is tame, this implies that every
G
-conjugacy class of
K
-minisotropic maximal
K
-tori contains a torus defined over
k
. If
\mathbf{G}
is not almost simple, then the example discussed in Remark
5.2.3
shows that there exists
x_{T}
such that
{\Fro}(x_{T})\neq x_{T}
.

Lemma 6.3.6
.

We have

(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\cong T_{F}\cdot(1-{\Fro})T/(1-{\Fro})T.

Remark 6.3.7
.

Since taking torsion points is left exact while
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})\cong((\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}})_{\tor}
and
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T)\cong(T_{{\Fro}})_{\tor}
, we conclude from Lemma
6.3.6
that the inclusion
T_{F}\rightarrow T
induces an injection
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})\rightarrow\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T)
.

Proof.

Since
T_{0}=(1-{\Fro})T_{0}
, we have
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\cong T_{F}/(1-{\Fro})T_{F}=(T_{F})_{{\Fro}}
.

Fix a strongly regular semisimple
\gamma\in T^{{\Fro}}
. As proved in Lemma
6.2.7
, the map
\lambda\colon\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}\rightarrow T_{F}/(1-{\Fro})T_{F}=(T_{F})_{{\Fro}}
descends to a bijective map
from
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}/\text{$G^{{\Fro}}$-conjugacy}
to
(T_{F})_{{\Fro}}
.
Define

\mu\colon T_{F}/(1-{\Fro})T_{F}\rightarrow T_{F}\cdot(1-{\Fro})T/(1-{\Fro})T

by
\mu(t(1-{\Fro})T_{F})=t(1-{\Fro})T
for
t(1-{\Fro})T_{F}\in(T_{F})_{\Fro}
. This map is surjective, and so the map
\tilde{\lambda}:=\mu\circ\lambda
from the set of
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
to
T_{F}\cdot(1-{\Fro})T/(1-{\Fro})T
is surjective. It will be enough to show that
\tilde{\lambda}
is injective.

Suppose
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{\gamma}
and
\tilde{\lambda}(\gamma_{1})=\tilde{\lambda}(\gamma_{2})
. After possibly conjugating by an element of
G^{{\Fro}}
, we can write
\gamma_{i}={{}^{k_{i}}\!}\gamma
with
k_{i}\in G_{F,0}
.
Since
\tilde{\lambda}(\gamma_{1})=\tilde{\lambda}(\gamma_{2})
, there exists
s\in T
such that

(k_{1}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{1}))(s^{-1}{\Fro}(s))=k_{2}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{2})
.
Note that
\gamma_{1}={{}^{k_{1}sk_{2}^{-1}}\!}\gamma_{2}
and
{\Fro}(k_{1}sk_{2}^{-1})=k_{1}sk_{2}^{-1}
, so
\gamma_{1}
is
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugate to
\gamma_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 6.3.8
.

The sequence

1\longrightarrow(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\longrightarrow(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\longrightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}}\longrightarrow 1

is exact.

Proof.

Since

1\longrightarrow\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}\longrightarrow T/T_{0}\longrightarrow\Omega\longrightarrow 1

is an exact sequence of
{\Fro}
-modules and taking coinvariants is right exact, it is enough to check that the map
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}
is injective. Note that by exactness the image of
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}
in
(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}
is
\ker((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}})
.

We first show that
T_{F}\cdot(1-{\Fro})T/(1-{\Fro})T=\ker((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}})
.

“
\supset
” Suppose
t\in T
such that the image of
t
in
(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}
belongs to
\ker((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}})
. As in the proof of Lemma
6.3.2
we may choose
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
kt\in\Stab_{G}(C)
. Since the image of
t
in
(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}
belongs to
\ker((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}})
and
(1-{\Fro})G_{F,0}=G_{F,0}
, we have
kt\in g^{-1}{\Fro}(g)G_{C,0}
for some
g\in\Stab_{G}(C)
. Again using Lemma
6.3.2
we can write
g=k^{\prime}s
where
k^{\prime}\in G_{F,0}
and
s\in T
. So,
kt\in s^{-1}(k^{\prime})^{-1}{\Fro}(k^{\prime}){\Fro}(s)G_{C,0}
. Since
G_{C,0}\leq G_{F,0}
and
{\Fro}(s)F=F
, after some manipulation we arrive at

ts{\Fro}(s^{-1})=sts{\Fro}(s^{-1})s^{-1}\in{{}^{s}\!}k^{-1}k^{\prime}{}^{-1}{\Fro}(k^{\prime}){{}^{{\Fro}(s)}\!}G_{C,0}\subset G_{F,0}.

Consequently,
t\in T_{F}\cdot(1-{\Fro})T
. Thus, since
(1-{\Fro})T_{0}=T_{0}
, we have that
\ker((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}})
is contained in

T_{F}\cdot(1-{\Fro})T/(1-{\Fro})T
.

“
\subset
”
On the other hand, if
\bar{t}=t(1-{\Fro})T
with
t\in T_{F}
, then from Lemma
6.3.2
we know that the image of
t
in
\Omega
is trivial.
Thus
T_{F}\cdot(1-{\Fro})T/(1-{\Fro})T=\ker((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}})
.

From Lemma
6.3.6
we conclude that
\ker((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}})
and
({T}_{F})_{\Fro}
have the same finite cardinality. The result follows.
∎

Remark 6.3.9
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is semisimple, then
the natural injections
T\rightarrow T
and
\Stab_{G}(C)\rightarrow G
induce isomorphisms

(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}=\tor((T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}})\cong\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T)\text{ and }\Omega_{{\Fro}}=\tor(\Omega_{{\Fro}})\cong\cohom^{1}({\Fro},G).

Under these isomorphisms, the map
(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow\Omega_{{\Fro}}
becomes
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T)\rightarrow\cohom^{1}({\Fro},G)
.
Since the cardinality of
\ker(\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T)\rightarrow\cohom^{1}({\Fro},G))
measures the number of rational classes in the
k
-stable class of any strongly regular semisimple element of
T^{\Fro}
,   Lemma
6.3.8
follows from Lemma
6.2.7
.

Corollary 6.3.10
.

\Stab_{G^{\Fro}}(F)=T^{{\Fro}}G_{F,0}^{{\Fro}}.

Remark 6.3.11
.

This can also be stated as
\Stab_{G^{\Fro}}(x_{T})=T^{{\Fro}}G_{x_{T},0}^{{\Fro}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
g\in\Stab_{G^{\Fro}}(F)
. From Lemma
2.3.1
we may choose
t\in T
and
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
g=tk
. Since
tk=g={\Fro}(g)={\Fro}(t){\Fro}(k)
, we conclude that
t^{-1}{\Fro}(t)\in T_{F}
. Since
t^{-1}{\Fro}(t)\cdot T_{0}
maps to the identity in
\Omega
, from Lemma
6.3.8
it is in the image of the map
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow(T/T_{0})_{{\Fro}}
.
Thus, we conclude that there exists
s\in T_{F}\leq G_{F,0}
such that
t^{-1}{\Fro}(t)=s^{-1}{\Fro}(s)
mod
T_{0}
; replacing
t
by
ts^{-1}
we conclude that
t^{-1}{\Fro}(t)\in T_{0}
. Since
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T_{0})
is trivial, there exists
r\in T_{0}
such that
t^{-1}{\Fro}(t)=r^{-1}{\Fro}(r)
. Replacing
t
by
tr^{-1}
, we conclude that
t\in T^{{\Fro}}
. Note that
g\in tG^{{\Fro}}_{F,0}
.
∎

Alternate Proof.

The short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow T_{F}\longrightarrow T\times G_{F,0}\longrightarrow\Stab_{G}(F)\longrightarrow 1

gives rise to the long exact sequence

1\longrightarrow T^{{\Fro}}_{F}\longrightarrow T^{{\Fro}}\times G^{{\Fro}}_{F,0}\longrightarrow\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(F)\longrightarrow\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T_{F})\longrightarrow\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T\times G_{F,0}).

Since
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T\times G_{F,0})\cong\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T)
and, by Remark
6.3.7
,
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T_{F})
injects into
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},T)
, the result follows.
∎

Definition 6.3.12
.

Set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}=\Ad({G_{x_{T},0}})\mathbf{T}
and let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}^{k}
denote the set of
{\Fro}
-stable tori in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}
.

Corollary 6.3.13
.

The injection
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{T}}^{k}
induces a bijection between the set of
G_{x_{T},0}^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}
and the set of
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T}}
.

Proof.

Since every
G_{x_{T},0}^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class in
\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}
determines a
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class in
\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T}}
, it is enough to show that if

\mathcal{C}
is a
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{T}}^{k}
, then
\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}
is a single
G_{x_{T},0}^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{T}}^{k}
. We first show that
\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}
is nonempty.
Choose
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. Let
x_{T^{\prime}}
denote the (unique) point in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)^{{\Fro}}
associated to
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}={{}^{g}\!}\mathbf{T}
for some
g\in G
, we conclude that
x_{T^{\prime}}=g\cdot x_{T}
. Since
G^{{\Fro}}
acts transitively on the alcoves in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)^{{\Fro}}
, there exists
h\in G^{{\Fro}}
such that
h\cdot x_{T^{\prime}}=x_{{{}^{h}\!}T^{\prime}}
belongs to
\bar{C}^{\prime{\Fro}}
. From Lemma
2.2.2
we conclude that
h\cdot x_{T^{\prime}}=hg\cdot x_{T}=x_{T}
.
Since
{{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}={{}^{hg}\!}\mathbf{T}
and
hg\cdot x_{T}=x_{T}
, from Lemma
2.3.1
we conclude that there exists
j\in G_{x_{T},0}
such that
{{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}={{}^{j}\!}\mathbf{T}
. That is,
\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}
is nonempty.

Suppose
\mathscr{C}_{1},\mathscr{C}_{2}\subset\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{O}^{k}_{\mathbf{T},x_{T}}
are two
G_{x_{T},0}^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes. Choose
\mathbf{T}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{i}
. Since
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists
g\in G^{{\Fro}}
such that
{{}^{g}\!}\mathbf{T}_{1}=\mathbf{T}_{2}
. Since
x_{T_{1}}=x_{T_{2}}=x_{T}
, we conclude that
g\in\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(x_{T})
. From Corollary
6.3.10
we conclude that there exists
j\in G_{x_{T},0}^{{\Fro}}
such that
{{}^{j}\!}\mathbf{T}_{1}=\mathbf{T}_{2}
. That is,
\mathscr{C}_{1}=\mathscr{C}_{2}
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.14
.

If
F=C
, then for every
{\Fro}
-stable facet
H
contained in the closure of
C
we can assume that the representatives for
\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(H)/G^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}
lie in
T^{{\Fro}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
H
is a
{\Fro}
-stable facet that is contained in the closure of
C
. If
h\in\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(H)
, then there exists
k\in G^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}
such that
hkC=C
. From Corollary
6.3.10
we can assume that
hk\in T^{{\Fro}}G^{\Fro}_{C,0}
. Thus,

hG^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}=hkG^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}\subset T^{{\Fro}}G^{{\Fro}}_{C,0}G^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}=T^{{\Fro}}G^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}.\qed

A similar proof shows:

Corollary 6.3.15
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is any
K
-minisotropic maximal
K
-torus, not assumed to be defined over
k
.
If
F=C
, then for every facet
H
contained in the closure of
C
we can assume that the representatives for
\Stab_{G}(H)/G_{H,0}
lie in
T
. ∎

6.4.
k
-stable conjugacy and
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy of tori

In this section we analyze the
{\Fro}
-structure of
{{}^{G}\!}T
where
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus. Recall that
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
denotes the set of
k
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
that are
G
-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}
.

Definition 6.4.1
.

If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{T}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
, then we will say that
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is
k
-stably-conjugate
to
\mathbf{T}^{\prime\prime}
provided that there is a
g\in G
such that
{{}^{g}\!}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}^{\prime\prime}
and
\Ad(g)\colon T^{\prime}\rightarrow T^{\prime\prime}
is a
k
-morphism. If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime},\mathbf{T}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
, we will say that
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugate
to
\mathbf{T}^{\prime\prime}
provided that there is a
g\in G^{{\Fro}}
such that
{{}^{g}\!}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}=\mathbf{T}^{\prime\prime}
.

Note that if two tori
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
are
k
-stably-conjugate, then there is an
x\in G
such that
{{}^{x}\!}(\mathbf{T}_{1}(k))=\mathbf{T}_{2}(k)
.

Remark 6.4.2
.

As in Definition
6.2.1
it would be more standard to define the notion of
k
-stably-conjugate in Definition
6.4.1
by taking
g\in\mathbf{G}(\bar{k})
. However, Lemma
2.1.1
allows us to restrict our attention to
g\in G
.

We first describe
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}/\text{$G^{{\Fro}}$-conjugacy}
, the set of rational conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
. For this we introduce the group
\bar{W}_{T}
and the notion of Frobenius conjugacy.

Definition 6.4.3
.

Set
W_{T}=N_{G}(\mathbf{T})/T
and
\bar{W}_{T}=N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})/T_{0}
where
T_{0}
is the parahoric subgroup of
T
.

Remark 6.4.4
.

Note that, in general,
\bar{W}_{T}
is not isomorphic to either
W_{T}=N_{G}(\mathbf{T})/T
or
N_{G}(\mathbf{T})/T_{F}
. Indeed, for
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
and
p>2
we have that

\bar{W}_{T}
has order four while both
W_{T}
and
N_{G}(\mathbf{T})/T_{F}=N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})/T_{F}
have order two.

Definition 6.4.5
.

Suppose
H
is a group on which
\langle{\Fro}\rangle
acts. Two elements
h,h^{\prime}\in H
are said to be
Frobenius conjugate
provided that there exists
x\in H
such that
x^{-1}h{\Fro}(x)=h^{\prime}
. We denote the set of Frobenius-conjugacy classes of
H
by
H_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}
.

Remark 6.4.6
.

If
H
is abelian, then
H_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}=H_{{\Fro}}
.

Lemma 6.4.7
.

There is a natural bijection between the set of
G^{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
and the set of
{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
\bar{W}_{T}
. That is

\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}/\text{$G^{{\Fro}}$-conjugacy}\,\,\longleftrightarrow\,\,(\bar{W}_{T})_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}.

Remark 6.4.8
.

Since taking coinvariants is right exact and
T_{0}=(1-{\Fro})T_{0}
, we have
(\bar{W}_{T})_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}\cong(N_{G_{F,0}}(T))_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
. From Lemma
6.1.3
we may assume, after conjugating by an element of
G^{{\Fro}}
, that
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
are conjugate, as
K
-tori, by an element of
\Stab_{G}({F})
. Thanks to Lemma
2.3.1
, we may assume that
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
are, in fact, conjugate by an element of
G_{F,0}
. Choose
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
{{}^{k}\!}\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
. Since both
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
are
k
-tori, they are
{\Fro}
-stable. Thus,
k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)\in N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})
. If
k^{\prime}\in G_{F,0}
is another element such that
{{}^{k^{\prime}}\!}\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
, then
m:=k^{-1}k^{\prime}\in N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})
and
(k^{\prime})^{-1}{\Fro}(k^{\prime})=m^{-1}(k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)){\Fro}(m)
. Consequently, after taking the quotient by
T_{0}
, we have a well-defined map
\rho\colon\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}\rightarrow(\bar{W}_{T})_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}
.

On the other hand, if
\bar{m}\in\bar{W}_{T}
, then we can choose
m\in N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})
lifting
\bar{m}
. Thanks to Lang-Steinberg, there is a
k\in G_{F,0}
such that
k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)=m
. Note that
{{}^{k}\!}\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
and
\rho({{}^{k}\!}\mathbf{T})
is the
{\Fro}
-conjugacy class of
\bar{m}
. That is,
\rho
is surjective.

To complete the proof, we need to show that if
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
, then
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}_{2}
if and only if
\rho(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\rho(\mathbf{T}_{2})
. As above, after possibly conjugating by an element of
G^{{\Fro}}
, we can write
\mathbf{T}_{i}={{}^{g_{i}}\!}\mathbf{T}
with
g_{i}\in{G_{F,0}}
.

Suppose
\rho(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\rho(\mathbf{T}_{2})
. Then there exist
m\in N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})
and
t\in T_{0}
such that

mg_{1}^{-1}{\Fro}(g_{1}){\Fro}(m)^{-1}=tg_{2}^{-1}{\Fro}(g_{2}).

Since
g_{2}tg_{2}^{-1}\in{{}^{g_{2}}\!}T_{0}
, by Lang-Steinberg, there is a
k\in{{}^{g_{2}}\!}T_{0}
such that
k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)=g_{2}tg_{2}^{-1}
. Thus,

{\Fro}(kg_{2}mg_{1}^{-1})=kg_{2}mg_{1}^{-1}

showing that
\mathbf{T}_{1}
and
\mathbf{T}_{2}
are conjugate by
kg_{2}mg_{1}^{-1}\in G^{{\Fro}}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is
G^{\Fro}
-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}_{2}
.
Then thanks to Corollary
6.3.10
with
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}_{1}
, there exists
h\in G^{{\Fro}}_{F,0}
such that
{{}^{h}\!}(\mathbf{T}_{1}(k))=\mathbf{T}_{2}(k)
. Thus, for all strongly regular semisimple
\gamma\in\mathbf{T}_{1}(k)
we have
{{}^{h}\!}\gamma\in\mathbf{T}_{2}(k)
. Since
{{}^{hg_{1}}\!}\mathbf{T}={{}^{g_{2}}\!}\mathbf{T}
, we have
m:=g_{2}^{-1}hg_{1}\in N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})
. Thus

g_{2}^{-1}{\Fro}(g_{2})=mg_{1}^{-1}h^{-1}{\Fro}(hg_{1}m^{-1})=mg_{1}^{-1}{\Fro}(g_{1}){\Fro}(m^{-1}).

We conclude that
\rho(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\rho(\mathbf{T}_{2})
.
∎

Remark 6.4.9
.

It would be nice if the intersection of a Frobenius-conjugacy class in
\bar{W}_{T}
with
\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}
would lie in
(1-{\Fro})\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}
, giving us an injective map
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\rightarrow(\bar{W}_{T})_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}
. However, this is not true. For example, when
p>2
and
-1\not\in(k^{\times})^{2}
consider a ramified elliptic maximal
k
-torus in
\SL_{2}
.

We wish to describe
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}/\!\approx
, the set of
k
-stable-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
.

Lemma 6.4.10
.

There is natural bijection between the set of
k
-stable conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
and the set of Frobenius conjugacy classes in
W_{T}=N_{G}(\mathbf{T})/T
.
That is

\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}/\!\approx\,\,\longleftrightarrow\,\,({W}_{T})_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}.

Proof.

Note that
W_{T}
may be naturally identified, as groups with
{\Fro}
-action, with
\bar{W}_{T}/\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}
.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.4.7
, we have a surjective, well-defined map
\mu\colon\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}\rightarrow(\bar{W}_{T}/\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{\sim_{{\Fro}}}
.

To complete the proof, we need to show that if
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
, then
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is
k
-stably-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}_{2}
if and only if
\mu(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\mu(\mathbf{T}_{2})
. Thanks to Lemma
2.3.1
and Lemma
6.1.3
, after conjugating by an element of
G^{{\Fro}}
, we can write
\mathbf{T}_{i}={{}^{k_{i}}\!}\mathbf{T}
with
k_{i}\in{G_{F,0}}
.

Suppose
\mu(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\mu(\mathbf{T}_{2})
. Then there exist
m\in N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})
and
t\in T_{F}
such that

mk_{1}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{1}){\Fro}(m^{-1})=t^{-1}k_{2}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{2}).

For all
\gamma\in\mathbf{T}_{1}(k)
we have

{\Fro}({{}^{k_{2}mk_{1}^{-1}}\!}\gamma)={{}^{k_{2}tmk_{1}^{-1}}\!}\gamma
. Since
{{}^{mk_{1}^{-1}}\!}\gamma\in\mathbf{T}(K)
, we have
{{}^{tmk_{1}^{-1}}\!}\gamma={{}^{mk_{1}^{-1}}\!}\gamma
. Thus,

{\Fro}({{}^{k_{2}mk_{1}^{-1}}\!}\gamma)={{}^{k_{2}mk_{1}^{-1}}\!}\gamma
, and we conclude that
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is
k
-stably-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}_{2}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}_{1}
is
k
-stably-conjugate to
\mathbf{T}_{2}
. Then there exists
h\in G_{F,0}
such that
{{}^{h}\!}(\mathbf{T}_{1}(k))=\mathbf{T}_{2}(k)
. Thus, for all strongly regular semisimple
\gamma\in\mathbf{T}_{1}(k)
we have
{{}^{h}\!}\gamma\in\mathbf{T}_{2}(k)
. Thus
s=h^{-1}{\Fro}(h)\in\mathbf{T}_{1}(K)={{}^{k_{1}}\!}(\mathbf{T}(K))
and
{{}^{k_{1}^{-1}}\!}s\in T_{F}
. Since
{{}^{hk_{1}}\!}\mathbf{T}={{}^{k_{2}}\!}\mathbf{T}
, we have
m:=k_{2}^{-1}hk_{1}\in N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})
. Thus

k_{2}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{2})=(mk_{1}^{-1}h^{-1}){\Fro}(hk_{1}m^{-1})=({{}^{mk_{1}^{-1}}\!}s)(mk_{1}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{1}){\Fro}(m^{-1})).

Since
{{}^{mk_{1}^{-1}}\!}s\in T_{F}
, we conclude that
\mu(\mathbf{T}_{1})=\mu(\mathbf{T}_{2})
.
∎

Different choices of “base point”
\mathbf{T}
for the orbit
{{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T}
will result in different realizations of the action of
{\Fro}
. We explore how these different realizations are related.

Definition 6.4.11
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
. Choose
h\in G
such that
T^{\prime}={{}^{h}\!}T
. Set
m_{h}=h^{-1}{\Fro}(h)
and let
\bar{m}_{h}
denote the image of
m_{h}
in
W_{T}
. Let
W_{T,\bar{m}_{h}}
denote the group
W_{T}
, but with
{\Fro}
acting by
{\Fro}\cdot x={{}^{\bar{m}_{h}}\!}{\Fro}(x)
. Similarly, we let
\bar{T}_{F,\bar{m}_{h}}
(resp.,
(T/T_{0^{+}})_{\bar{m}_{h}}
) denote the group
\bar{T}_{F}
(resp.
T/T_{0^{+}}
), but with
{\Fro}
acting by
{\Fro}\cdot t={{}^{\bar{m}_{h}}\!}{\Fro}(t)
.

Lemma 6.4.12
.

Recall that
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}={{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
with
h\in G
. Let
F^{\prime}
denote the facet corresponding to
T^{\prime}
.

(1)

The group homomorphism that sends
m^{\prime}\in N_{G}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
to
h^{-1}m^{\prime}h\in N_{G}(\mathbf{T})
induces a
{\Fro}
-equivariant isomorphism

\varphi_{h}\colon W_{T^{\prime}}\rightarrow W_{T,\bar{m}_{h}}.

(2)

The group homomorphism that sends
t^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}
to
h^{-1}t^{\prime}h\in T
induces
{\Fro}
-equivariant isomorphisms

\varphi_{h}\colon T^{\prime}/T^{\prime}_{0^{+}}\rightarrow(T/T_{0^{+}})_{\bar{m}_{h}}\text{ and }\varphi_{h}\colon\bar{T}^{\prime}_{F^{\prime}}\rightarrow\bar{T}_{F,\bar{m}_{h}}.\qed

6.5.
The rational structure of
{{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T}
: a summary

In this subsection we gather some of the key results and definitions of the previous subsections. Recall that
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus, and
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
denotes the set of tori in
{{}^{G}\!}\mathbf{T}
that are defined over
k
.

•

k
-stable classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
. Two tori
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
are said to be
k
-stably-conjugate provided that there is an
x\in G
such that
{{}^{x}\!}t_{1}\in T_{2}^{\Fro}
for all
t_{1}\in T_{1}^{\Fro}
. The
k
-stable classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
are parameterized by
(W_{T})_{{\sim_{{\Fro}}}}
, the set of Frobenius-conjugacy classes in
N_{G}(\mathbf{T})/T
. (Lemma
6.4.10
)

•

k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
. If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
, then a
k
-embedding of
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
into
\mathbf{G}
is a
k
-morphism
f\colon\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
that arises via conjugation for some
x\in G
. Up to
G^{\Fro}
-conjugation, the set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
is parameterized by

(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{{\Fro}}
, the group of
{\Fro}
-coinvariants in
\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}=(T\cap G_{F,0})/T_{0}
. If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
belongs to the stable class indexed by
w\in c\in(W_{T})_{{\sim_{{\Fro}}}}
, then the set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
into
\mathbf{G}
is parameterized by
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F})_{w{\Fro}}
, the group of
w{\Fro}
-coinvariants in
\bar{\mathsf{T}}_{F}
. (Lemmas
6.2.7
and
6.4.12
)

•

G^{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
. The set of
G^{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
is parameterized by
(\bar{W}_{T})_{{\sim_{{\Fro}}}}
, the set of Frobenius-conjugacy classes in
\bar{W}_{T}=N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})/T_{0}
.
(Lemma
6.4.7
)

6.6.
A comment on the relationship between the normalizer of
\mathbf{T}
over
K
and the normalizer of
\mathbf{T}
over
k_{\dtame}

We now check that the parahoric subgroup
G_{F,0}
interacts with the normalizer of
\mathbf{T}
as expected.

Lemma 6.6.1
.

If
E
is a Galois extension of
K
over which
\mathbf{T}
splits and
y
is the
\Gal(E/K)
-fixed point of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T},E)
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},E)
, then

N_{G}(\mathbf{T})\cap G_{F,0}\subset\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0}.

If
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame, then

N_{G}(\mathbf{T})\cap G_{F,0^{+}}=T\cap G_{F,0^{+}}=T_{0^{+}}.

Proof.

Suppose
m\in N_{G}(\mathbf{T})\cap G_{F,0}
.
Our first goal is to show that
m\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0}
. Note that
x_{T}
is the unique closest point in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(\mathbf{G},K)
to
y
. Similarly,
y
is the unique point in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T},E)
closest to
x_{T}
. Indeed, the unique point, call it
z
, in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T},E)
nearest
x_{T}
has the property that
\dist(x_{T},z)=\dist(\gamma(x_{T}),\gamma(z))=\dist(x_{T},\gamma(z))
for all
\gamma\in\Gal(E/K)
. Thus, by uniqueness, we conclude that
z
is
\Gal(E/K)
fixed; hence it must be
y
.

Since
y
is the unique
\Gal(E/K)
-fixed point in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T},E)
,
my\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T},E)
, and
\gamma(my)=my
for all
\gamma\in\Gal(E/K)
, we conclude that
my=y
.
From
[
3
, Lemma 2.12]
, we have
m\in G_{F,0}\leq\mathbf{G}(E)_{F,0}
, and so
from
[
14
, Lemma 4.2.1]
we conclude that
m\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0}
.

For the second claim, it is enough to show
N_{G}(\mathbf{T})\cap G_{F,0^{+}}\subset T
. Choose
m\in N_{G}(\mathbf{T})\cap G_{F,0^{+}}

Note that
m\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0}\cap\mathbf{G}(E)_{x_{T},0^{+}}
, which means that the image of
m
in the reductive quotient
\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0}/\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0^{+}}
must be unipotent. Since
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-tame, we can assume
E
is a tame extension of
K
and so the image of
m
in
\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0}/\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0^{+}}
is semisimple. Thus, the image of
m
is the trivial element.
Consequently,
m
fixes an open neighborhood
U
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T},E)
with
y\in\bar{U}
, hence
m
must fix all of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{T},E)
and so
m\in\mathbf{T}(E)
. Since
m\in N_{G}(\mathbf{T})\cap\mathbf{T}(E)
, we conclude that
m\in T
.
∎

Remark 6.6.2
.

The proof of Lemma
6.6.1

shows that, in fact,
N_{G}(\mathbf{T})\leq N_{\mathbf{G}(E)_{y,0}}(\mathbf{T})\cdot T
.

Remark 6.6.3
.

Tameness may or may not be required for the second statement of Lemma
6.6.1
. However, it is required for the current proof. For example, for
{\mathfrak{f}}
of characteristic two we have

\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

in
\SL_{2}({\mathfrak{f}})
. That is, without some assumptions on the characteristic of our field, nontrivial elements of the normalizer of a torus can be unipotent!

Remark 6.6.4
.

Thanks to Lemma
6.6.1
when
\mathbf{T}
splits over a tame extension, we can interpret
N_{G_{F,0}}(\mathbf{T})/(T\cap G_{F,0^{+}})
as
\Stab_{\mathsf{G}_{F}}(\mathbf{T})
where
\mathsf{G}_{F}
is the connected reductive
{\mathfrak{f}}
-group corresponding to the quotient
G_{F,0}/G_{F,0^{+}}
. By
\Stab_{\mathsf{G}_{F}}(\mathbf{T})
we mean the group of
\bar{x}\in\Stab_{\mathsf{G}_{F}}(\mathbf{T})
for which there exists a lift
x\in G_{F,0}
such that
{{}^{x}\!}\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}
.

7.
The Main Result and Examples

We begin by stating the parameterization theorem of this paper. We then develop some machinery for calculating the objects that appear in this theorem, and we end by looking at a number of examples.

7.1.
The main result

Before stating the main result, we develop and recall some notation.

Let
(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}
denote the set of
{\Fro}\circ N_{q}
-fixed points in the set of
\sigma
-elliptic tame
\sigma
-conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
. For
c\in(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}
, choose
w\in c
and let
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
be a lift of
w
. Thanks to Remark
3.3.8
it makes sense to denote the order of
n\sigma\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\rtimes\tilde{\Gamma}
by
\ell_{c}
and to
define
K_{c}={k_{\dtame}}^{\sigma^{\ell_{c}}}
.
Let
\pi
denote a uniformizer in the tame extension of
K
of degree
\ell_{c}
such that
\pi^{\ell_{c}}=\varpi
. Let
\lambda_{c}
denote the unique element in
\X_{*}(\mathbf{A}\cap\mathbf{G}^{\prime})
such that

•

n
is
\sigma
-conjugate to
\lambda_{c}(\sigma(\pi)/\pi)
by an element of
\tilde{G}_{x_{0},0}
(see Lemma
3.2.5
) and

•

x_{c}:=x_{0}+\lambda_{c}/\ell_{c}
is an element of
\bar{C}^{\prime}
(see Lemma
3.5.1
).

If
c\in(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}
, then there exists a tame
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus
\mathbf{T}^{c}
in
\mathbf{G}
for which the point associated to
T^{c}
is
x_{c}
(see Lemma
3.3.6
and Corollaries
3.3.4
and
5.1.3
). Set
T^{c}_{x_{c}}=T^{c}\cap G_{x_{c},0}
.

Theorem 7.1.1
.

Recall that
k
is a nonarchimedean local field. For each
c\in(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}
fix a tame
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus
\mathbf{T}^{c}
in
\mathbf{G}
for which the point associated to
T^{c}
is
x_{c}
.

(1)

The set of
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes of tame
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
is parameterized by the set

\{(c,\tilde{c})\,|\,\text{ $c\in(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}$ and $\tilde{c}\in(\bar{W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}$}\}.

Here
(\bar{W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}
denotes the set of
{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
\bar{W}_{T^{c}}:=N_{G_{x_{c},0}}(\mathbf{T}^{c})/T^{c}_{0}
.
More precisely, for each
\tilde{c}\in(\bar{W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}
we fix

k_{\tilde{c}}\in G_{x_{c},0}
such that
k_{\tilde{c}}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{\tilde{c}})\in N_{G_{x_{c},0}}(\mathbf{T}^{c})
is a lift of a member of
\tilde{c}
. Then each
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T^{c}}
has exactly one representative in the set

\{\Ad({k_{\tilde{c}}})\mathbf{T}^{c}\,|\,\tilde{c}\in(\bar{W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}\}.

(2)

The set of
k
-stable classes of tame
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
is parameterized by the set

\{(c,\hat{c})\,|\,\text{ $c\in(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}$ and $\hat{c}\in({W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}$}\}.

Here
({W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}
denotes the set of
{\Fro}
-conjugacy classes in
{W}_{T^{c}}:=N_{G_{x_{c},0}}(\mathbf{T}^{c})/T^{c}_{x_{c}}
.
More precisely, for each
\hat{c}\in({W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}
we fix

k_{\hat{c}}\in G_{x_{c},0}
such that
k_{\hat{c}}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{\hat{c}})\in N_{G_{x_{c},0}}(\mathbf{T}^{c})
is a lift of a member of
\hat{c}
. Then each
k
-stable conjugacy class in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T^{c}}
has exactly one representative in the set

\{\Ad({k_{\hat{c}}})\mathbf{T}^{c}\,|\,\hat{c}\in({W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}\}.

(3)

Suppose
c\in(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}
and
\hat{c}\in({W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}
. Let
\mathbf{T}=\Ad({k_{\hat{c}}})\mathbf{T}^{c}
where
k_{\hat{c}}
is chosen as in (
2
). Let
\bar{m}
denote the image of
k_{\hat{c}}^{-1}{\Fro}(k_{\hat{c}})
in
{W}_{T^{c}}
. The set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
is parameterized by
(\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{c}_{x_{c}})_{\bar{m}{\Fro}}
, the group of
\bar{m}{\Fro}
-coinvariants in

\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{c}_{x_{c}}=T^{c}_{x_{c}}/T^{c}_{0}
. More precisely, for each
\bar{c}\in(\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{c}_{x_{c}})_{\bar{m}{\Fro}}
we fix
k_{\bar{c}}\in G_{x_{c},0}
such that
k_{\bar{c}}^{-1}(\bar{m}{\Fro}(k_{\bar{c}})\bar{m}^{-1})\in T^{c}_{x_{c}}
is a lift of a member of
\bar{c}
.
Then

\{f_{\bar{c}}\,\colon\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}\,|\,\text{ $f_{\bar{c}}(s)=\Ad({k_{\hat{c}}k_{\bar{c}}k_{\hat{c}}^{-1}})s$ for $s\in\mathbf{T}$ and $\bar{c}\in(\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{c}_{x_{c}})_{\bar{m}{\Fro}}$}\}

is a complete set of representatives for the
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
.

Remark 7.1.2
.

If
\hat{c}\in({W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}
, then the fiber over
\hat{c}
of the natural map

(\bar{W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}\rightarrow({W}_{T^{c}})_{\sim{\Fro}}

parameterizes the set of
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes of tame
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-tori that appear in the
k
-stable class corresponding to
\hat{c}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
3.4.1
every
G
-conjugacy class of tame maximal
K
-tori in
\mathbf{G}
corresponds to an element of
\tilde{W}^{\dtame}_{\sim}
. Thus the assignment
c\mapsto{{}^{G}\!}{\,}\mathbf{T}^{c}
from
(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}
to the set of
G
-conjugacy classes of tame
K
-minisotropic maximal tori in
\mathbf{G}
has image consisting of exactly those
G
-conjugacy classes of tame
K
-minisotropic maximal tori that contain a torus defined over
k
.

Consequently, to establish statements (
1
) and (
2
) of the theorem it is enough to show that for every
c\in(\tilde{W}^{\dtame,\ee}_{\sim})^{{\Fro}\circ N_{q}}
the
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy classes and
k
-stable classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T^{c}}
can be parameterized as claimed. This follows from Lemmas
6.4.7
and
6.4.10
.

Statement (
3
) follows from Corollary
6.2.9
and Lemma
6.4.12
.
∎

7.2.
A recasting of part (
1
) of Theorem
7.1.1

In Corollary
7.2.4
below we recast part (
1
) of Theorem
7.1.1
in a way that does not require the choice of a base point
\mathbf{T}^{c}
. While perhaps more elegant from the point of view of avoiding choices, this new description is considerably less concrete.

7.2.1.
A result about nearby tori

We begin with a result about tori that are, in some sense, close to each other at depth zero.

Lemma 7.2.1
.

For this lemma we let
E
be any extension of
k
and let
{\mathfrak{f}}_{E}
denote its residue field. Suppose
x\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G},E)
and
\mathsf{T}
is a maximal
{\mathfrak{f}}_{E}
-torus in the connected reductive
{\mathfrak{f}}_{E}
-group
\mathsf{G}^{E}_{x}
corresponding to the quotient
\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0}/\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0^{+}}
.
Suppose
\mathbf{T}_{1},\mathbf{T}_{2}
are
E
-split maximal tori such that
x\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{i},E)
.

•

If the image of
\mathbf{T}_{i}(E)\cap\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0}
in
\mathsf{G}^{E}_{x}
is
\mathsf{T}
, then
\mathbf{T}_{1}
and
\mathbf{T}_{2}
are
\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0^{+}}
-conjugate.

•

If for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
k\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,n}
such that
{{}^{k}\!}\mathbf{T}_{1}=\mathbf{T}_{2}
, then
\mathbf{T}_{1}=\mathbf{T}_{2}
.

•

If
g\in N_{\mathbf{G}(E)}(\mathbf{T}_{1})\cap\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0^{+}}
, then
g\in\mathbf{T}_{1}(E)_{0^{+}}
.

Proof.

Since
x
belongs to
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{1},E)\cap\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{2},E)
, there exists an
h\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0}
such that
{{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}_{1}=\mathbf{T}_{2}
. Let
\bar{h}
denote the image of
h
in

\mathsf{G}^{E}_{x}
. By hypothesis,
{{}^{\bar{h}}\!}\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{T}
; thus,
\bar{h}\in N_{\mathsf{G}^{E}_{x}}(\mathsf{T})
. Consequently, by looking
at the affine Bruhat decomposition, we see that there exist an
n\in N_{\mathbf{G}(E)}(\mathbf{T}_{1})\cap\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0}
and a
g\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0^{+}}
such that
h=gn
. We have
\mathbf{T}_{2}={{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}_{1}={{}^{g}\!}\mathbf{T}_{1}
. This establishes the first claim.

For the second claim, suppose
\mathbf{T}_{1}\neq\mathbf{T}_{2}
. Then there exists
t\in\mathbf{T}_{1}(E)
such that
t\not\in\mathbf{T}_{2}(E)
.
Since
\mathbf{T}_{2}(E)
is closed, there exists
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that
t\cdot\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,\ell}\cap\mathbf{T}_{2}(E)=\emptyset
.
By hypothesis, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
k\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,n}
such that
{{}^{k}\!}t\in\mathbf{T}_{2}(E)
. This implies that
t\in\mathbf{T}_{2}(E)
modulo
\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,m}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. This contradicts the fact that
t\cdot\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,\ell}\cap\mathbf{T}_{2}(E)=\emptyset
.

For the final claim we note that since
g\in N_{\mathbf{G}(E)}(\mathbf{T}_{1})
, we have that
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{1},E)
is stabilized by
g
. If C is an alcove in
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{1},E)
such that
F\subset\bar{C}
, then since
g\in\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0^{+}}
we have that

g
fixes
C
pointwise. Therefore, since
g\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{1},E)=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{1},E)
and
g
fixes
C
pointwise, we conclude that
g
fixes
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{T}_{1},E)
pointwise. Thus, we conclude
that
g\in\mathbf{T}_{1}(E)\cap\mathbf{G}(E)_{x,0^{+}}=\mathbf{T}_{1}(E)_{0^{+}}
.
∎

7.2.2.
Recasting part (
1
) of Theorem
7.1.1

The point
x_{c}
of Theorem
7.1.1
determines a facet
F_{c}
in
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G},K_{c})
. Let
\mathsf{G}_{c}
denote the connected reductive
\mathfrak{F}
-group attached to
F_{c}
. We identify
\mathsf{G}_{c}
with
\mathbf{G}(K_{c})_{F_{c},0}/\mathbf{G}(K_{c})_{F_{c},0^{+}}
, and we have
\mathsf{G}_{c}^{\sigma}=\mathsf{G}_{x_{c}}
. (Recall that
\mathsf{G}_{c}^{\sigma}
denotes the connected component of
\Fix_{\mathsf{G}_{c}}(\sigma)
.) We let
