-stable class there is, up to
\SU_{3}(k)
-conjugacy, only one
k
-embedding of
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
into
\SU_{3}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{O}^{k}_{c_{w_{0}}}
. Then
{W}_{T}
is isomorphic to
S_{3}
, the symmetric group on three letters. Since
\tilde{W}^{-1}=\tilde{W}
, we can assume
w_{\Fro}=1
. Thus

\mathcal{O}^{k}_{c_{w_{\alpha}}}
decomposes into three
k
-stable classes – one for each
{\Fro}
-conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}^{-1}=\tilde{W}
.
We have
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}{}^{-1}
is
\mu_{2}\times\mu_{2}
.
If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
belongs to a
k
-stable class that is indexed by a
{\Fro}
-conjugacy class with one element then, up to
\SU_{3}(k)
-conjugacy,
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
has four
k
-embeddings into
\SU_{3}
.
If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
belongs to a
k
-stable class that is indexed by a
{\Fro}
-conjugacy class with three elements then, up to
\SU_{3}(k)
-conjugacy,
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
has two
k
-embeddings into
\SU_{3}
.
If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
belongs to a
k
-stable class that is indexed by a
{\Fro}
-conjugacy class with two elements then, up to
\SU_{3}(k)
-conjugacy,
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
has one
k
-embedding into
\SU_{3}
.

8.
K
-minisotropic tori in isogenous groups

Motivated by the examples of
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
and
{\mathrm{PSp}}_{4}
discussed in Examples
7.8
and
7.9
we show that information about
K
-minisotropic maximal tori can, in some cases, be derived from information about the analogous tori in isogenous groups.

8.1.
On the surjectivity of isogenies at the level of parahoric groups for tori

Recall that
L
is the completion of
K
and
R_{L}
denotes its ring of integers. Let
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
be tori over
K
, hence they are tori over
L
. We denote by
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
the connected Néron models of
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
, such that
\bar{T}_{0}:=\mathcal{T}(R_{L})\subset\bar{T}:=\mathbf{T}(L)
is the parahoric subgroup of
\bar{T}
and
\bar{T}^{\prime}_{0}:=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(R_{L})\subset\bar{T}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{T}^{\prime}(L)
is the parahoric subgroup of
\bar{T}^{\prime}
.

Suppose
\rho:\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is an isogeny.
Since
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
are (lft-)Néron models,
\rho
extends uniquely to a morphism
\rho:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}

[
7
, Proposition 6]
.
From
[
25
, Proposition B.9.1]
, we have

\bar{T}_{0^{+}}:=\mathcal{T}(R_{L})_{0^{+}}=\ker(\mathcal{T}(R_{L})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F}))
and
\bar{T}^{\prime}_{0^{+}}:=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(R_{L})_{0^{+}}=\ker(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(R_{L})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F}))
.

Proposition 8.1.1
.

If
\rho:\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is an isogeny whose order is prime-to-
p
, then
\rho[\bar{T}_{0}]=\bar{T}^{\prime}_{0}
,
\rho[\bar{T}_{0^{+}}]=\bar{T}^{\prime}_{0^{+}}
, and
\rho
is surjective on special fibers.

Proof (Cheng-Chiang Tsai).

We first claim that
C:=\coker(\bar{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{\rho}\bar{T}_{0}^{\prime})
is finite and prime-to-
p
. Indeed, we have a Snake Lemma diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\rho_{*})_{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{T}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{T}^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{T}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})_{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(L,\ker(\rho))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\bar{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\bar{T}^{\prime}}}

where
\ker(\rho_{*})
and
H^{1}(L,\ker(\rho))
are both finite and prime-to-
p
, hence the claim.

Next, we claim that
\rho:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is surjective on special fibers. Suppose on the contrary that it is not. Since
\mathfrak{F}
is infinite, we then have

\coker(\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F}))
is infinite. Since
\bar{T}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(R_{L})
surjects onto
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F})
, we have that
C
is also infinite, a contradiction.

Looking again at a Snake Lemma diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathcal{T}(R_{L})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(R_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(R_{L})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(R_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coker(f^{\flat})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\flat}}

we need to show that
\rho^{\flat}
is surjective. Since we already know that
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F})
surjects onto
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F})
and that both
C
and
\ker(\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F}))
are finite and prime-to-
p
, it suffices to show that
\coker(f^{\flat})
is pro-
p
. This follows from the fact that
\ker(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(R_{L})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{F}))
is pro-
p

[
25
, Proposition A.4.23]
.
∎

Corollary 8.1.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
are tori that are defined over
K
. If
\rho:\mathbf{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is an isogeny whose order is prime-to-
p
, then

\rho[T_{0}]=T^{\prime}_{0}
and
\rho[{T}_{0^{+}}]={T}^{\prime}_{0^{+}}
.

Proof (Cheng-Chiang Tsai).

Suppose
\gamma^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}_{0}
. From Proposition
8.1.1
there exists
\gamma\in\bar{T}_{0}=\mathcal{T}(R_{L})
such that
\rho(\gamma)=\gamma^{\prime}
. The fiber of
\rho
over
\gamma^{\prime}
is a finite scheme over
K
, and so any element of the fiber must be defined over a finite extension, call it
K^{\prime}
,   of
K
. Thus,
\gamma\in\mathbf{T}(K^{\prime})\cap\mathbf{T}(L)=\mathbf{T}(K)
. We conclude that
\gamma\in T_{0}
. A similar argument shows that
\rho[{T}_{0^{+}}]={T}^{\prime}_{0^{+}}
.
∎

8.2.
On the surjectivity of isogenies at the level of parahoric subgroups

Suppose
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{L}
are reductive
K
-groups and
\rho\colon\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}
is a
K
-isogeny.
Note that
\rho
carries
H
into
L
and, for
x\in{\mathcal{B}}(H)={\mathcal{B}}(L)
,
it carries
\Stab_{H}(x)
into
\Stab_{L}(x)
. Thus, from
[
23
, Corollary 3.3.1]
the map
\rho
carries
H_{x,0}
into
L_{x,0}
.
We show that, under mild conditions, the latter map is surjective.

A version of the lemma below, but for
k
rather than
K
, occurs in
[
23
, Lemma 3.3.2]
.

Lemma 8.2.1
.

If
p
does not divide the order of
\ker(\rho)
, then the map
\res_{H_{x,0^{+}}}\rho\colon H_{x,0^{+}}\rightarrow L_{x,0^{+}}
is surjective. Similarly, the map

\res_{H_{x,0}}\rho\colon H_{x,0}\rightarrow L_{x,0}

is surjective.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{H}
denote a maximal
K
-split torus in
\mathbf{H}
such that
x
belongs to the apartment of
A_{H}
. Let
\mathbf{A}_{L}
denote the corresponding maximal
K
-split torus of
\mathbf{L}
.
Let
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{H}
denote the centralizer of
\mathbf{A}_{H}
in
\mathbf{H}
, and let
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{L}
denote the centralizer of
\mathbf{A}_{L}
in
\mathbf{L}
. Since
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{L}
are
K
-quasi-split, both
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{H}
and
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{L}
are maximal
K
-tori. Moreover,
\rho({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{H})={\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{L}
.

Since the restriction of
\rho
to the group generated by unipotent elements in
H_{x,0^{+}}
is an isomorphism onto the group generated by unipotent elements in
L_{x,0^{+}}
, it is enough to show that the restriction of
{\rho}
to the pro-unipotent radical of
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{H}
surjects onto the pro-unipotent radical of
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{L}
. This follows from Corollary
8.1.2
.

Similarly, since the restriction of
\rho
to the group generated by unipotent elements in
H_{x,0}
is an isomorphism onto the group generated by unipotent elements in
L_{x,0}
, it is enough to show that the restriction of
{\rho}
to the parahoric subgroup of
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{H}
surjects onto the parahoric subgroup of
{A}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{L}
. This follows from Corollary
8.1.2
.
∎

8.3.
k
-embeddings of maximal
K
-anisotropic
k
-tori in isogenous semisimple groups

Suppose now that
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{L}
are semisimple
k
-groups and
\rho\colon\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{L}
is a
k
-isogeny.

Let
\mathbf{T}^{H}
be a maximal
K
-minisotropic
k
-torus in
\mathbf{H}
, and let
\mathbf{T}^{L}
be the corresponding maximal
K
-minisotropic
k
-torus in
\mathbf{L}
. Let
x_{T}
denote the point in
{\mathcal{B}}(L)={\mathcal{B}}(H)
identified by
\mathbf{T}
and let
F
denote the facet to which
x_{T}
belongs. From Remark
2.3.6

it follows that
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[T^{L}_{F}]
, the preimage of
T^{L}_{F}=T^{L}\cap L_{x_{T},0}
under
\res_{H}\rho
, is
T^{H}_{F}
and
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[N_{L_{x_{T},0}}(T^{L})]=N_{H_{x_{T},0}}(T^{H})
.

Set
Z_{F}^{H}=Z^{H}\cap H_{F,0}
where
Z^{H}
denotes the center of
H
. Similarly, since
\ker(\rho)\leq\mathbf{Z}^{H}
it makes sense to define
\ker(\rho)_{F}=\ker(\res_{H}\rho)\cap H_{F,0}
.

Lemma 8.3.1
.

If
T^{L}_{0}
denotes the parahoric subgroup of
T^{L}
, then
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[T^{L}_{0}]\leq T^{H}_{F}
and

(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[T^{L}_{0}]=\ker(\rho)_{F}T^{H}_{0}.

Proof.

Since
T^{L}_{0}\leq T^{L}_{F}
and
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[T^{L}_{F}]=T^{H}_{F}
, we have
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[T^{L}_{0}]\leq T^{H}_{F}
.

Since
L
is semisimple, we have that
T^{L}_{0}=T^{L}_{0^{+}}.
Thus, if

t\in(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[T^{L}_{0}]
, then
\bar{t}
, the image of
t
in
L
, is topologically unipotent and so
t
must be topologically unipotent mod
\ker(\res_{H}\rho)
. Since
(\res_{H}\rho)^{-1}[T^{L}_{0}]\leq T^{H}_{F}
, we conclude that
t
must be topologically unipotent mod
\ker(\rho)_{F}
.
∎

Definition 8.3.2
.

Define
W^{H}_{T}=N_{H}(\mathbf{T}^{H})/T^{H}=N_{H_{x_{T},0}}(T^{H})/T^{H}
,
\bar{W}^{H}_{T}=N_{H_{x_{T},0}}(T^{H})/T^{H}_{0}
, and
\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{H}=T^{H}_{F}/T^{H}_{0}
. Define
W^{L}_{T}
,
\bar{W}^{L}_{T}
, and
\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{L}
similarly.

Lemma 8.3.3
.

The map
\rho
induces an isomorphism
W^{H}_{T}\cong W^{L}_{T}
. Moreover,
if
p
does not divide the order of
\ker(\rho)
, then
\rho
induces surjections
N_{H_{x_{T},0}}(T^{H})\twoheadrightarrow N_{L_{x_{T},0}}(T^{L})
,
T^{H}_{F}\twoheadrightarrow T^{L}_{F}
,
\bar{W}^{H}_{T}\twoheadrightarrow\bar{W}^{L}_{T}
, and
\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{H}\twoheadrightarrow\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{L}
. The kernel of each of these surjections is
\ker(\rho)_{F}
or
\ker(\rho)_{F}T^{H}_{0}/T^{H}_{0}
as appropriate.

Proof.

Since
\ker(\res_{H}\rho)\leq T^{H}
, we have
W^{H}_{T}\cong W^{L}_{T}
.
The remainder of the statements follow from Corollary
8.1.2
and Lemmas
8.2.1
and
8.3.1
.
∎

Remark 8.3.4
.

If
p
does not divide the order of
\ker(\rho)
, then
we may and do identify
\ker(\rho)_{F}
with its image in
\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{H}
.

Corollary 8.3.5
.

If
p
does not divide the order of
\ker(\rho)
, then

(T^{L}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\cong(T^{H}_{F})_{{\Fro}}/(\ker(\rho)_{F})_{\Fro}\text{\, \, and \, \,}(\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{L})_{{\Fro}}\cong(\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{H})_{{\Fro}}/(\ker(\rho)_{F})_{\Fro}.

Proof.

From Lemma
8.3.3
we know that

1\rightarrow\ker(\rho)_{F}\longrightarrow T^{H}_{F}\longrightarrow T^{L}_{F}\longrightarrow 1

is exact.
Since taking coinvariants is right exact, we have

(T^{L}_{F})_{{\Fro}}\cong(T^{H}_{F})_{{\Fro}}/(\ker(\rho)_{F})_{\Fro}
.
Similarly, from Lemma
8.3.3
we know that

1\rightarrow\ker(\rho)_{F}T^{H}_{0}/T^{H}_{0}\longrightarrow\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{H}\longrightarrow\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{L}\longrightarrow 1

is exact. Since taking coinvariants is right exact, from Lemma
8.3.1
we conclude

(\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{L})_{{\Fro}}\cong(\bar{\mathsf{T}}^{H})_{{\Fro}}/(\ker(\rho)_{F})_{\Fro}
.
∎

Corollary 8.3.6
.

If
p
does not divide the order of
\ker(\rho)
, then

N_{L_{x_{T},0}}(T^{L})_{\sim{\Fro}}\cong(N_{H_{x_{T},0}}(T^{H})/\ker(\rho)_{F})_{\sim{\Fro}}\text{\, \, and \, \,}(\bar{W}^{L}_{T})_{\sim{\Fro}}\cong(\bar{W}^{H}_{T}/\ker(\rho)_{F})/_{\sim{\Fro}}.\qed

Remark 8.3.7
.

If
z\in\ker(\rho)_{F}
and
c\in(\bar{W}^{H}_{T})_{\sim{\Fro}}
, then, since
z
is central in
H
, we have
cz\in(\bar{W}^{H}_{T})_{\sim{\Fro}}
.
For
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
in
(\bar{W}^{H}_{T})_{\sim{\Fro}}
we write
c_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\sim}}c_{2}
provided that there exists
z\in\ker(\rho)_{F}
such that
c_{1}=c_{2}z
. Then
(\bar{W}^{H}_{T}/\ker(\rho)_{F})/_{\sim{\Fro}}
may be identified with the set of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\sim}}
-equivalence classes in
(\bar{W}^{H}_{T})_{\sim{\Fro}}
. A similar interpretation holds for
(N_{H_{x_{T},0}}(T^{H})/\ker(\rho)_{F})_{\sim{\Fro}}
.

Remark 8.3.8
.

Suppose
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
is the derived group of
\mathbf{G}
. Recall that
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}
denotes the simply connected cover of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
while
\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
denotes the adjoint group of
\mathbf{G}
. Let
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
denote a
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
.

The results of this Section (that is, Section
8.3
) show that if
p
does not divide the order of the kernel of the isogeny
\mathbf{G}_{sc}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, then the stable classes in the
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
-orbit of
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
, the rational classes in the
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
-orbit of
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
, and the
k
-embeddings, up to rational conjugacy, of
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
into
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
can be calculated in a straightforward manner if we know how to parameterize these things for the corresponding torus in
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}
.

8.4.
A generalization of Example
7.6

If
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split and
\mathbf{T}
is a tame
K
-minisotropic maximal
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
corresponding to the Coxeter element of
\tilde{W}
, then from Example
7.6
the set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
form a single
G_{\ad}^{\Fro}
-orbit. This phenomenon can be generalized as follows.

Lemma 8.4.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal
K
-minisotropic
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
. Let
x_{T}
denote the point in
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
identified by
\mathbf{T}
and let
F
denote the facet to which
x_{T}
belongs.
If the images of
T\cap G_{F,0}
and
Z\cap G_{F,0}
in
\mathsf{G}_{F}
agree, then
G_{\ad}^{{\Fro}}
acts transitively on the set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
. In fact, up to
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy, the parahoric subgroup
((G_{\ad})_{F,0})^{{\Fro}}
acts transitively on the set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{T}_{\ad}
denote the
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
corresponding to
\mathbf{T}
.

The
k
-map
\mathbf{G}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
induces maps
G_{F,0}\rightarrow(G_{\ad})_{F,0}
and
G_{F,0^{+}}\rightarrow(G_{\ad})_{F,0^{+}}
. Since the images of
T\cap G_{F,0}
and
Z\cap G_{F,0}
in
\mathsf{G}_{F}
agree, we conclude that the image of
T_{\ad}\cap(G_{\ad})_{F,0}
in
(\mathsf{G}_{\ad})_{F}
is trivial.

Suppose
\gamma\in T^{{\Fro}}
is strongly regular semisimple and
g\in G
such that
{{}^{g}\!}\gamma
is Frobenius fixed. Since
{{}^{g}\!}\gamma
is Frobenius fixed, we conclude that
g\cdot x_{T}
is Frobenius fixed. Hence, from Lemma
6.1.4
there exists
\ell\in G^{{\Fro}}
such that
\ell g\cdot x_{T}=x_{T}
. So, without loss of generality we may and do assume
g\in\Stab_{G}(x_{T})
. It will be enough to show that there exists
h\in((G_{\ad})_{F,0})^{{\Fro}}
such that
{{}^{g}\!}\gamma={{}^{h}\!}\gamma
.

Let
\bar{g}
denote the image of
g
in
G_{\ad}
. Note that
\bar{g}\in\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(x_{T})
. From Lemma
2.3.1
there exist
t\in T_{\ad}
and
k\in(G_{\ad})_{F,0}
such that
\bar{g}=kt
. Thus,
{{}^{g}\!}\gamma={{}^{k}\!}\gamma
. But then
k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)\in T_{\ad}\cap(G_{\ad})_{F,0}\leq T_{\ad}\cap(G_{\ad})_{F,0^{+}}=(T_{\ad})_{0}
. Since
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},(T_{\ad})_{0})
is trivial, there exists
y\in(T_{\ad})_{0}
such that
k^{-1}{\Fro}(k)=y^{-1}{\Fro}(y)
. But then
h=ky^{-1}\in((G_{\ad})_{F,0})^{\Fro}\leq G_{\ad}^{\Fro}
and
{{}^{h}\!}\gamma={{}^{k}\!}\gamma={{}^{g}\!}\gamma
.
∎

9.
K
-minisotropic Coxeter tori

The existence of a maximal
K
-minisotropic
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
was established by Adler in the Appendix to
[
13
]
. We sharpen this result by showing that there exists a maximal
K
-minisotropic
k
-torus such that
x_{T}
is in the interior of
C^{\prime}
.

Lemma 9.0.1
.

Recall that
Z_{C}=Z\cap G_{C,0}
. There exists a maximal
K
-minisotropic
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
such that (a)
x_{T}\in C^{\prime}
and (b)
T_{C}=Z_{C}T_{0^{+}}
.

Proof.

If
\psi
is an affine root of
\mathbf{A}
with respect to
\mathbf{G}
,
\mathbf{A}
,
K
, and our valuation
\nu
, then we let
U_{\psi}
denote the subgroup of
U_{\dot{\psi}}
corresponding to
\psi
. Recall that for
z\in\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{A})
, we have
U_{\psi}\leq G_{z,0}
if and only if
\psi(z)\geq 0
.

Write
\Phi
as the finite disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}\Phi_{i}
with each
\Phi_{i}
irreducible. This decomposition produces a decomposition
\Delta=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}\Delta_{i}
of the affine simple roots and a decomposition
\Psi=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}\Psi_{i}
of the affine roots. Similarly, we also have a decomposition
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)={\mathcal{B}}(G_{\scon})=\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\mathcal{B}}(G^{i}_{\scon})
where
G^{i}_{\scon}
is a simply connected group with root system
\Phi_{i}
, and we write
C^{\prime}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}C^{\prime}_{i}
for the corresponding decomposition of
C^{\prime}
. For each
1\leq i\leq m
there exist unique
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and a unique
x_{i}\in C^{\prime}_{i}
such that
r_{i}=\psi(x_{i})
for all
\psi\in\Delta_{i}
. Let
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{m})\in C^{\prime}
and
\vec{r}=(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{m})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}
. Let
G_{x,\vec{r}}
be the group
\langle A_{0^{+}},U_{\psi}\,|\,\text{$\psi\in\Psi_{i}$ implies $\psi(x_{i})\geq r_{i}$}\rangle
and let
G_{x,\vec{r}^{+}}
be the group
\langle A_{0^{+}},U_{\psi}\,|\,\text{$\psi\in\Psi_{i}$ implies $\psi(x_{i})>r_{i}$}\rangle
.

Since
{\Fro}
preserves
C
, it acts on
\Delta
.
For
\psi\in\Delta
, choose
u_{\psi}\in U_{\psi}\setminus U_{\psi^{+}}
such that
{\Fro}(u_{\psi})=u_{{\Fro}(\psi)}
.
Set
u=\prod u_{\psi}
.

Suppose
y=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{m})\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
. If for each
1\leq i\leq m
we have
\psi(y_{i})=r_{i}
for all
\psi\in\Delta_{i}
, then
x=y
. That is,
u\in G_{y,\vec{r}}\setminus G_{y,\vec{r}^{+}}
if and only if
x=y
. In fact, this is true for any element of
uG_{x,\vec{r}^{+}}
.

It follows that
C_{G}(\gamma)\leq\Stab_{G}(C)
for all
\gamma\in uG_{x,\vec{r}^{+}}
. Since
u^{-1}{\Fro}(u)\in G_{x,\vec{r}^{+}}
and
\cohom^{1}({\Fro},G_{x,\vec{r}^{+}})
is trivial, we conclude that
uG_{x,\vec{r}^{+}}\cap G^{{\Fro}}\neq\emptyset
. Thus, we may choose a strongly regular
\gamma\in uG_{x,\vec{r}^{+}}\cap G^{{\Fro}}
. Since
C_{G}(\gamma)
is a bounded subgroup of
G
, we conclude that
\mathbf{T}:=C_{\mathbf{G}}(\gamma)
is a maximal
K
-anisotropic
k
-torus of
\mathbf{G}
. Note that
x_{T}=x
.

Suppose
t\in T_{F}=G_{x_{T},0}\cap T
, and let
\bar{t}
denote the image of
t
in
\mathsf{G}_{x_{T}}
. Since
x_{T}
is in
C^{\prime}
, the reductive quotient
\mathsf{G}_{x_{T}}
is
\mathsf{A}
. Since
t
commutes with
\gamma\in uG_{x_{T},\vec{r}^{+}}
and
\bar{t}\in\mathsf{A}
, we have
\alpha(\bar{t})=1
for all
\alpha\in\Delta
. Thus,
T_{C}=Z_{C}T_{0^{+}}
.
∎

The point
x_{T}
for
\mathbf{T}=C_{\mathbf{G}}(\gamma)
with strongly regular
\gamma
as in the proof of Lemma
9.0.1
is,
in the
K
-split tame setting, given by the Kac coordinates of the Coxeter conjugacy class in
\tilde{W}
. For this reason, we make the following definition.

Definition 9.0.2
.

A torus that arises via the construction in the proof of Lemma
9.0.1
is called a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
k
-torus
. If we don’t require that it be defined over
k
(i.e., we don’t require that
{\Fro}(u)=u
or
{\Fro}(\gamma)=\gamma
), then we will call it a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
K
-torus
or just a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter torus
.

K
-minisotropic Coxeter
k
-tori have many desirable properties. We enumerate some of these properties here.

Corollary 9.0.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
k
-torus with
x_{T}\in C^{\prime}
.

•

If a facet
H
of
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
is contained in the closure of
C
, then the representatives for
\Stab_{G}(H)/G_{H,0}
can be chosen in
T
. If
H
is also
{\Fro}
-stable, then the representatives for
\Stab_{G^{{\Fro}}}(H)/G^{{\Fro}}_{H,0}
can be chosen in
T^{{\Fro}}
.

•

The group
G_{\ad}^{{\Fro}}
acts transitively on the set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}
into
\mathbf{G}
.

•

Every element of
\Stab_{G_{\ad}}(C)
is cohomologous to an element of
T_{\ad}
. Here
\mathbf{T}_{\ad}
is the
k
-torus in
\mathbf{G}_{\ad}
corresponding to
\mathbf{T}
.

Proof.

The first point follows from Corollaries
6.3.14
and
6.3.15
, the second follows from
Lemma
8.4.1
, and the final point follows from Lemma
2.3.1
and Remark
3.4.5

∎

Recall that
\eta\colon\mathbf{G}_{\scon}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
is the composition
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
.

Corollary 9.0.4
.

If
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
K
-torus and
x_{T}\in C^{\prime}
, then
T_{C}=\eta[Z_{\scon}]T_{0}
.

Proof.

We already know from Corollary
2.3.4
that
T_{C}=\eta[T_{\scon}]T_{0}
. Since
\mathbf{T}_{\scon}
is a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}_{\scon}
, from Lemma
9.0.1
we have
T_{\scon}=(T_{\scon})_{C}=Z_{\scon}\cdot(T_{\scon})_{0^{+}}
where
Z_{\scon}
denotes the center of
G_{\scon}
. Since
\eta[(T_{\scon})_{0^{+}}]\subset T_{0}
, the claim follows.
∎

Corollary 9.0.5
.

If
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
, then
A\cap T=A\cap Z
.

Remark 9.0.6
.

This result is not true for arbitrary
K
-minisotropic tori. For example, if
p>2
and
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic torus in
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
of type
-1\in A_{1}\times A_{1}
as in Example
3.6.2
, then
A\cap T
has order
4
, not
2
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may and do assume that
\mathbf{G}
is absolutely almost simple.

Choose
g\in A\cap T
. Let
\mathbf{H}=C_{\mathbf{G}}(g)^{\circ}
. If
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{G}
, then
g\in Z
.

Suppose
\mathbf{H}\neq\mathbf{G}
. Note that
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}\leq\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{T}\leq\mathbf{H}
.

If
\mathbf{H}
is a Levi
K
-subgroup, then since
g\in A
we must have that
\mathbf{H}
is the Levi of a parabolic
K
-subgroup. However, since
\mathbf{T}
is elliptic in
\mathbf{G}
, it cannot then belong to
\mathbf{H}
.

Thus,
\mathbf{H}
must be a generalized Levi
K
-subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
that is not the Levi of a parabolic
K
-subgroup. Consequently, the ranks of the derived groups of
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
must be the same and so we can identify
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(H)
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
.
Since
\mathbf{T}\leq\mathbf{H}
, by uniqueness we have
x_{T}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(H)\subset{\mathcal{B}}^{\red}(G)
. Choose
r>0
so that
G_{x_{T},0^{+}}=G_{x_{T},r}\neq G_{x_{T},r^{+}}
. Since
x_{T}\in{\mathcal{B}}(H)
, there exists
h\in H
such that
hx_{T}\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)
. Let
C_{1}^{\prime}
be the
G
-alcove in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)
that contains
hx_{T}
, let
x_{1}
be an absolutely special vertex in
\bar{C}^{\prime}_{1}
, and let
\mathbf{B}_{1}
be the Borel subgroup determined by
\mathbf{A}
,
\bar{C}_{1}^{\prime}
, and
x_{1}
.
Since
{{}^{h}\!}\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
, it contains an element
u
that, modulo
G_{hx_{T},r^{+}}
, looks like
\prod u_{\psi}
as
\psi
runs over
{\Delta}^{\prime}=\Delta(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A},K,\nu,C_{1}^{\prime})
and
u_{\psi}\in U_{\psi}\setminus U_{\psi^{+}}
.
Since
H_{y,s}=H\cap G_{y,s}
for all
y\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A)
and
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have that, modulo
H_{hx_{T},r^{+}}
, the element
u\in H
looks like
\prod u_{\psi}
where
\psi
runs over
{\Delta}^{\prime}
and
u_{\psi}\in U_{\psi}\setminus U_{\psi^{+}}
. We conclude that for each
\psi\in{\Delta}^{\prime}
we have
\dot{\psi}\in\Phi(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{A})
. In particular,
\Delta(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})
is a subset of
\Phi(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{A})
, hence
\Phi(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{A})=\Phi(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{A})
. This contradicts our assumption that
\mathbf{H}\neq\mathbf{G}
.
∎

Remark 9.0.7
.

The proof of Corollary
9.0.5
shows that if
\mathbf{H}
is a connected reductive
K
-subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
that contains both a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter torus and the centralizer of a maximal
K
-split torus of
\mathbf{G}
, then
\mathbf{H}
must be
\mathbf{G}
. In particular, the intersection of a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter torus and
any
maximal
K
-split torus of
\mathbf{G}
must be central.

As Lemma
9.0.8
shows, the Lie algebra version of Corollary
9.0.5
holds for any
K
-minisotropic Cartan subalgebra. Thus, in characteristic zero we can always conclude that if
\mathbf{G}
is semisimple and
\mathbf{T}
is any
K
-minisotropic torus
\mathbf{G}
, then
A\cap T
is finite.

Lemma 9.0.8
.

Suppose
k
is any field of characteristic zero and
\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of a reductive
k
-quasi-split group. Suppose
\mathfrak{b}
is a Borel
k
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{h}
is a Cartan
k
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{b}
. If
\mathfrak{t}
is a
k
-elliptic Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, then
\mathfrak{t}\cap\mathfrak{h}^{k}
is
\mathfrak{z}\cap\mathfrak{h}^{k}
, where
\mathfrak{z}
denotes the center of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Here
\mathfrak{h}^{k}
denotes the maximal
k
-split toral subalgebra of
\mathfrak{h}
.

Proof.

Suppose
Y\in\mathfrak{t}\cap\mathfrak{h}^{k}
.
Then
C_{\mathfrak{g}}(Y)
is a Levi
k
-subalgebra of a parabolic
k
-subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. Since
\mathfrak{h}\leq C_{\mathfrak{g}}(Y)
and
\mathfrak{h}
is
k
-elliptic, we conclude that
C_{\mathfrak{g}}(Y)=\mathfrak{g}
. That is,
Y\in\mathfrak{z}
.
∎

Remark 9.0.9
.

In characteristic zero, the proof of Lemma
9.0.1
can be modified to work on the Lie algebra by choosing, for
\psi\in\Delta
,
X_{\psi}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\psi}\setminus{\mathfrak{g}}_{\psi^{+}}
such that
{\Fro}(X_{\psi})=X_{{\Fro}(\psi)}
. Here the subgroups
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\psi}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\psi^{+}}
of the root group
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\dot{\psi}}
are the Lie algebra analogues of
U_{\psi}
and
U_{\psi^{+}}
. We then replace
\gamma
with a regular semisimple
Y\in X+{\mathfrak{g}}^{{\Fro}}_{x,r^{+}}
where
X=\sum X_{\psi}\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{{\Fro}}
.

Remark 9.0.10
.

In the tame setting the
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class of
C_{\mathbf{G}}(\gamma)
is independent of the choice of strongly regular
\gamma\in uG^{{\Fro}}_{x,r^{+}}
. Similarly, in the tame setting in characteristic zero the
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy class of the Cartan subalgebra
C_{{\mathbf{\mathfrak{g}}}}(Y)
is independent of the choice of regular semisimple
Y\in X+{\mathfrak{g}}^{{\Fro}}_{x,r^{+}}
. This follows from
[
2
, Lemma 2.3.2]
.

Appendix A
Two questions about parahoric subgroups of
K
-minisotropic tori

Mitya Boyarchenko, Stephen DeBacker, Anna Spice, Loren Spice, and Cheng-Chiang Tsai

We continue to use the notation developed in the main body of this paper. In particular,
\mathbf{G}
is a connected reductive
k
-group,
G
is the group of
K
-points of
\mathbf{G}
,
C
is an alcove in the building of
G
, and
G_{C,0}
is the parahoric subgroup of
G
corresponding to
C
. If
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-torus, then
{\mathcal{B}}(T)
is the alcove of
{\mathcal{B}}(T)
, and we denote by
T_{0}
the parahoric subgroup of
T
.

We explore two natural questions that arise from results in this paper:

(1)

Does the sequence

(A.0.1)

1\longrightarrow G_{C,0}\longrightarrow\Stab_{G}(C)\longrightarrow\Stab_{G}(C)/G_{C,0}\longrightarrow 1

discussed in Section
6.3
always split?

(2)

If
\mathbf{T}
is a maximal
K
-anisotropic torus in the derived group of
\mathbf{G}
, is the cardinality of
T/T_{0}
independent of the isogeny class of the derived group?

A.1.
On the splitting of
A.0.1

It is known that the sequence
A.0.1
splits whenever
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split and either the derived group of
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected or
\mathbf{G}
is almost simple
[
4
]
.
In this appendix we show that
A.0.1
also
splits when both
\mathbf{G}
is adjoint and a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
k
-torus (see Definition
9.0.2
) in
\mathbf{G}
splits over a tame extension of
K
. We then show that
A.0.1
does not, in general, split.

Suppose
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-anisotropic maximal torus. Note that
T
is bounded.
Write
T=T_{p^{\prime}}\times T_{p}
where
T_{p^{\prime}}
is the subgroup of elements of order coprime to
p
and
T_{p}
is the pro-
p
-subgroup of
T
. If
T_{p}=T_{0}=T_{0^{+}}
, which always happens when
\mathbf{T}
splits over a tame extension, then the sequence

1\longrightarrow T_{0}\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow T/T_{0}\longrightarrow 1

splits.

Lemma A.1.1
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is an adjoint group and
\mathbf{T}
is a
K
-minisotropic Coxeter
K
-torus in
\mathbf{G}
that splits over a tame extension, then sequence
A.0.1
splits.

Proof.

This follows from the discussion prior to the statement of the lemma together with Corollary
6.3.15
and Lemmas
6.3.2
and
9.0.1
.
∎

To see that the sequence in
A.0.1
can fail to split in the absence of tameness, even in characteristic zero, consider the following example. Let
k
be
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, let
i\in\bar{k}
be a square root of
-1
, and let
E
be the quadratic ramified extension
K[i]
. Note that
1+i
is a uniformizer in
E
. Let
\mathbf{T}
be a
K
-torus such that
\mathbf{T}(E)=E^{\times}
and
T=N_{E/K}^{1}
, the kernel of the norm map from
E
to
K
. From
[
28
, Section 7.3]
we have the following commutative diagram.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\times}=\mathbf{T}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{E/K}^{1}=T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{\Gal(E/K)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\mathbf{T}(E)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{T}}

Here each of the horizontal maps is a surjective Kottwitz map,
N
is defined by
N(x)=x\tau({x})^{-1}
where
\tau
is the nontrivial element of
\Gal(E/K)
, and
\alpha
is the obvious surjective map. Since
N(1+i)=i
and
\alpha(\kappa_{\mathbf{T}(E)}(1+i))\neq 0
in
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{\Gal(E/K)}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\kappa_{T}(i)\neq 0
. Note that
\kappa_{T}(-1)=\kappa_{T}(i^{2})=2\kappa_{T}(i)=0
. Since the kernel of
\kappa_{T}
is
T_{0}
, we conclude that
-1\in T_{0}
and
\bar{i}
, the image of
i
in
T/T_{0}
, generates the group
T/T_{0}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. Since the only order two element of
T
is
-1
, we conclude that the sequence in
A.0.1
does not split.

If we replace
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
in the above discussion with
\mathbb{F}_{2}((t))
where
\mathbb{F}_{2}
is the field with two elements and let
E
be a quadratic extension of
\mathbb{F}_{2}((t))
, then
T/T_{0}
has order two, but
T
has no order two elements and so the sequence in
A.0.1
does not split.

A.2.
Isogeny and the cardinality of
T/T_{0}
for
K
-anisotropic
\mathbf{T}

If
\mathbf{T}
is a
k
-torus in
\SL_{2}
corresponding to the norm one elements of a tamely ramified extension and
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is the corresponding
k
-torus in
\PGL_{2}
, then
T/T_{0}
and
T^{\prime}/T_{0}^{\prime}
have the same cardinality. This is not a general phenomenon, but it is not rare.

From the results of Haines and Rapoport and of Kottwitz discussed in Section
2.3
we have
T/T_{0}\cong\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I}
where
I
is the inertial group
\Gal(\bar{k}/K)
. We will study
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I}
.

Lemma A.2.1
.

Let
\Lambda
be an additive cyclic group of order
n
, and

\tau
an automorphism of
\Lambda
.
Let
m
be a multiple of
n
such that

\tau^{m}
is trivial,
and put

N_{\tau,m}(\lambda)\mathrel{:=}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\tau^{i}(\lambda)

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Then
N_{\tau,m}
is identically
0
on
\Lambda
.

Proof.

By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, we may, and do, assume that
n
is a power of some prime
\ell
, so that
\Lambda
is a cyclic module for the
\ell
-adic integers
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
. Since
N_{\tau,m}
factors through
N_{\tau^{m/n},n}
, we may, and do, assume that
m
is also a power of
\ell
, as well as a multiple of
n
(or even that
m
equals
n
, though we do not need this).

Choose an element
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
such that
\tau
sends a generator
\lambda
of
\Lambda
to
k\lambda
, hence acts as multiplication by
k
on all of
\Lambda
. Then
N_{\tau,m}
acts on
\Lambda
as multiplication by the
\ell
-adic integer
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}k^{i}
.

Since
\tau^{m}
, which acts on
\Lambda
as multiplication by
k^{m}
, is trivial, and the annihilator of
\Lambda
in
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
is
n\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
, we have that
k^{m}
belongs to
1+n\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
. Since
m
is a power of
\ell
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}/(1+\ell\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
has order relatively prime to
\ell
, we conclude that
k
belongs to
1+\ell\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
, and so that the
\ell
-adic valuation of
k^{m}-1
is the sum of the
\ell
-adic valuations of
m
and
k-1
.
That is,
k^{m}-1
belongs to
m(k-1)\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
, so
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}k^{i}=\frac{k^{m}-1}{k-1}
belongs to
m\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}\subseteq n\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}
, and hence multiplication by
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}k^{i}
annihilates
\Lambda
; that is,
N_{\tau,m}
is identically
0
on
\Lambda
.
∎

We now present a technical result, Proposition
A.2.2
, on Weyl groups, whose proof seems to require case-by-case analysis.

The quantification in Proposition
A.2.2
seems elaborate, but note that almost every irreducible root system has a cyclic fundamental group, so the result usually says that the order of
w
is divisible by the order of the fundamental group, and in the remaining case (of
D_{n}
with
n
even) just says that the order of
w
is divisible by
2
.

Proposition A.2.2
.

If
\Phi
is an irreducible root system and
w
is an elliptic element of
W(\Phi)
, then the order of
w
is divisible by the order of every element of every subquotient of the fundamental group of
\Phi
.

Proof.

Put
W=W(\Phi)
. We indicate below the possibilities, according to the Borel–de Siebenthal algorithm, for a maximal proper, but non-parabolic, Weyl subgroup
W^{\prime}
of
W
.

For
W
of type
A_{n}
, the maximal proper Weyl subgroups of
W
are all parabolic. Since the Weyl groups of types
B_{n}
and
C_{n}
are the same, we do not distinguish them.

We put
B_{1}=C_{1}=A_{1}
and
D_{2}=A_{1}+A_{1}
, but do not consider
D_{1}
, so that any occurrence of a symbol
D_{m}
carries the implicit constraint
m>1
.

Type of
W

Type of
W^{\prime}

B_{n}
or
C_{n}

C_{r}+C_{n-r}
,
D_{n}

D_{n}

D_{r}+D_{n-r}

E_{6}

A_{1}+A_{5}
,
3A_{2}

E_{7}

A_{1}+D_{6}
,
A_{2}+A_{5}
,
A_{1}+2A_{3}
,
A_{7}

E_{8}

A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{5}
,
A_{1}+A_{7}
,
A_{1}+E_{7}
,
A_{2}+E_{6}
,
A_{3}+D_{5}
,
2A_{4}
,
A_{8}
,
D_{8}

F_{4}

A_{1}+A_{3}
,
A_{1}+C_{3}
,
2A_{2}
,
B_{4}

G_{2}

A_{1}+A_{1}
,
A_{2}
.

Recall that
W
is the Weyl group of
\Phi
.
For each entry in the chart above,
if
W^{\prime}
is the Weyl group of
\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
is an irreducible component of
\Phi^{\prime}
, then
in each case except
D_{n}
with
n
even, the fundamental group of
\Phi
is (cyclic and) isomorphic to a subgroup of the fundamental group of
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
. Even in this exceptional situation, we have that every cyclic subgroup of the fundamental group of
\Phi
is isomorphic to a subgroup of the fundamental group of
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
.

Therefore, we may, and do, assume that
w
belongs to no proper Weyl subgroup of
W
. Then, using
[
11
, p. 8, Lemma 10]
to enumerate the possibilities and
[
11
, p. 23, Table 3]
to compute their orders, we find:

Type of
W

order of
w
is divisible by …

A_{n}

n+1

B_{n}
or
C_{n}

2n

D_{n}

2\gcd(n-1,2)

E_{6}

3

E_{7}

2

E_{8}

1

F_{4}

12

G_{2}

6
.

The result follows.
∎

Lemma A.2.3
.

Suppose
k
has characteristic zero and

\mathbf{G}
is split and simple over
K
. Suppose
\rho\colon\mathbf{G}\rightarrow\mathbf{H}
is an isogeny. Let
\mathbf{T}
be a
K
-anisotropic maximal torus in
\mathbf{G}
and let
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
denote the corresponding torus in
\mathbf{H}
under
\rho
. Let
E
be the splitting field of
\mathbf{T}
over
K
, and suppose that
I_{E}:=\Gal(E/K)
is cyclic.
We have that
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I_{E}}
and
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})_{I_{E}}
have the same (finite) cardinality.

Proof.

Since
k
has characteristic zero, the isogeny is separable.
Since
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split and
K
-simple, its absolute root system is irreducible.

By assumption,
I_{E}
is cyclic, say of order
m
. Choose a generator
\tau
.

We have an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{*}}

and
\Lambda
is a quotient of the (absolute) fundamental group
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})/\mathbb{Z}\Phi^{\vee}(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
of
\mathbf{H}
, hence a subquotient of the fundamental group of the absolute root system
\Phi(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
. The short exact sequence above yields the long exact sequence in homology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cohom_{1}(I_{E},\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cohom_{1}(I_{E},\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})_{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Since
I_{E}
is cyclic of order
m
with generator
\tau
, for every
\mathbb{Z}
-module
A
on which
I_{E}
acts we have
\cohom_{1}(I_{E},A)=A^{I_{E}}/N_{I_{E}}[A]
where
N_{I_{E}}(a)=\sum_{j=0}^{(m-1)}\tau^{j}(a)
for
a\in A
. Since
\mathbf{T}
, and so
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
, is
K
-anisotropic, we conclude that

\cohom_{1}(I_{E},\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime}))=0
. Thus, the long exact sequence becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{I_{E}}/N_{I_{E}}[\Lambda]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})_{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{I_{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-\tau}

is exact, we conclude that
\left|\Lambda^{I_{E}}\right|=\left|\Lambda_{I_{E}}\right|
.
Thus, the index of
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I_{E}}
in
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})_{I_{E}}
is
\left|N_{I_{E}}[\Lambda]\right|
.

Since
\mathbf{G}
, hence
\mathbf{H}
, is
K
-split, it must be the case that there is some elliptic element
w\in\tilde{W}
such that the action of
\Gal(E/K)
on
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
satisfies
\tau(\lambda)=w(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
. Then
m
is the order of
w
, so Proposition
A.2.2
gives that the order of every element of
\Lambda
divides
m
.

Moreover, the action of
I_{E}
on the fundamental group of the absolute root system
\Phi(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{T}^{\prime})
, hence on its subquotient
\Lambda
, is trivial.
Thus
N_{I_{E}}(\Lambda)
is multiplication by
m
, which, by assumption, divides the order of every element of
\Lambda
; so
N_{I_{E}}(\Lambda)
equals
0
.
∎

Remark A.2.4
.

If
I_{E}
is not cyclic, then the cardinality of

\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I_{E}}
and
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})_{I_{E}}
need not be the same, even if
\Lambda
is cyclic. For example, suppose that
k
has characteristic zero, the residue field of
K
has characteristic
3
, and
E
is a Galois extension of
K
with Galois group
S_{3}
. Let
\mathbf{T}
be the maximal
k
-torus in
\mathrm{SL}_{3}

determined by the standard action of
S_{3}
on

\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})=\{\sum a_{i}e_{i}\,|\,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ with }a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}=0\}.

Here
(e_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq 3)
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
Specifically,
\mathbf{T}
may be taken to be
\ker(\operatorname{Res}_{E^{\prime}/k}\operatorname{GL}_{1}\to\operatorname{GL}_{1})
, where
E^{\prime}
is the fixed field of any one of the order-
2
elements of
I_{E}
.
Let
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
denote the corresponding
k
-torus in
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}
under the isogeny
\rho\colon\mathrm{SL}_{3}\rightarrow\mathrm{PGL}_{3}
. Then
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T})_{I_{E}}
is trivial while
\X_{*}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime})_{I_{E}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
.

Appendix B
On
-1
invariants and Tits groups of a split simply connected group

Ram Ekstrom

We continue to use the notation of the main body of this paper. Suppose
\mathbf{G}
is a simply connected
K
-split group. This means that
\tilde{K}=K
and
\bar{\sigma}=1
. Since
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected,
\X_{*}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
is spanned by the coroots,
{\Phi}^{\vee}=\Phi^{\vee}(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
.

Suppose
-1
belongs to
\tilde{W}
and let
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
be a representative of
-1
. In this note we investigate when the group of
n
-fixed points of
N=N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
is a Tits group (with respect to some pinning). We assume
n
is tame. Thanks to the next lemma, this is equivalent to assuming
p
is not two.

Lemma B.0.1
.

n^{4}=1
.

Proof.

Since
(-1)^{2}=1
, we know
n^{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\cap{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. Thus, there exists
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}{\Phi}^{\vee}
such that
n^{2}=\mu(-1)
. The result follows.
∎

Let
\ell
denote the order of
n
; so
\ell
is
2
or
4
.
Let
\xi
be
-1
or
i
depending on whether
\ell
is two or four.

B.1.
The structure of
N^{nt}
for any
t\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}

Recall that
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected and
K
-split.
Let
N=N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
.

Lemma B.1.1
.

For all
t\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
, the elements
n
and
nt
have the same order. In fact, we have
(nt)^{2}=n^{2}
.

Remark B.1.2
.

Since
n
is elliptic,
n
and
nt
are conjugate by an element of
{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. Hence
n
and
nt
have the same order.

Proof.

Note that

(nt)^{2}={{}^{n}\!}t\cdot{{}^{n^{2}}\!}t\cdot n^{2}=t^{-1}\cdot t\cdot n^{2}=n^{2}.

Since
n
has order 2 or 4, the result follows.
∎

Lemma B.1.3
.

Fix
t\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}_{0}
.
There is a pinning
(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B},\{X^{t}_{a}\}_{a\in\tilde{\Pi}})
such that if

n_{a}^{t}
is the unique element of

N\,\bigcap\,\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})\exp(X^{t}_{a})\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})=N\,\bigcap\,\exp(X^{t}_{a})\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})\exp(X^{t}_{a})

for
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
, then

N^{nt}=\langle n^{t}_{a}\colon a\in\tilde{\Pi}\rangle.

That is,
N^{nt}
is the Tits group for the pinning
(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B},\{X^{t}_{a}\}_{a\in\tilde{\Pi}})
.

Proof.

If
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
, then

\displaystyle{{}^{nt}\!}n_{a}=\check{a}(a(t))^{-1}\cdot{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}.

Recall that
n_{a}
is the unique element of
N\cap\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})\exp(X_{a})\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})
. Because
{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}
and
n_{a}
have the same image in
\tilde{W}
and
n
normalizes
\mathbf{G}_{a}
, we have that
n_{a}^{-1}\cdot{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}
lies in both
\tilde{G}_{a}\cap{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}\cap{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. So
n_{a}^{-1}\cdot{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}
is in the image of
\check{a}
and has order either one or two. In the former case
n_{a}
is fixed by
n
while in the latter
{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}=n_{a}\check{a}(-1)
.
If
{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}=n_{a}
, then
define
y_{a}^{t}\in R_{K}
to be a square root of
a(t)
. If
{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}=n_{a}\check{a}(-1)
, then define
y_{a}^{t}
to be a square root of
-a(t)
.
Observe that these elements exist since the polynomial
X^{2}-a(t)
has roots in
R_{\tilde{K}}^{\times}=R_{K}^{\times}
. This is because, modulo
\wp_{\tilde{K}}
,
X^{2}-a(t)
has simple roots over the algebraically closed residue field,
p
is not equal to two, and
\tilde{K}
is Henselian. Put
X^{t}_{a}=(y_{a}^{t})^{-1}\cdot X_{a}
and note that
n^{t}_{a}={{}^{\check{a}(f_{a}^{t})}\!}n_{a}=n_{a}\check{a}(y^{t}_{a})
where
f_{a}^{t}
is a square root of
(y_{a}^{t})^{-1}
. We then calculate that if
{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}=n_{a}
, then

\displaystyle{{}^{nt}\!}n^{t}_{a}=\check{a}(a(t))^{-1}\cdot n_{a}\cdot\check{a}(y_{a}^{t})^{-1}=n_{a}\cdot(\check{a}(a(t))\cdot\check{a}(y_{a}^{t})^{-1})=n_{a}\check{a}(y^{t}_{a})=n_{a}^{t}.

While if
{{}^{n}\!}n_{a}=n_{a}\check{a}(-1)
, then we similarly find

\displaystyle{{}^{nt}\!}n_{a}^{t}=n_{a}\cdot(\check{a}(a(t))\cdot\check{a}(-1)\cdot\check{a}(y_{a}^{t})^{-1})=n_{a}\check{a}(y_{a}^{t})=n_{a}^{t}.

Consequently the group
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{t}=\langle n^{t}_{a}\colon a\in\tilde{\Pi}\rangle
is a Tits group for the pinning
(\mathbf{G},{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}},\mathbf{B},\{X^{t}_{a}\}_{a\in\tilde{\Pi}})
and is fixed by
nt
. Since
N=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{t}{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected, we find

N^{nt}=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{t}\cdot({\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})^{nt}=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{t}\cdot({\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})^{n}=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{t}.\qed

Remark B.1.4
.

Note that
N^{n}=\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}
, and it can happen that
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}^{1}\neq\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
.

Remark B.1.5
.

Simply connectedness is necessary in the above lemma. For example for the non-simply connected group
\mathbf{G}=\PGL_{2}
with
{\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
the diagonal torus, consider the matrices

n=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},\,t_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}.

Then
n=n_{a}
is a nontrivial representative of the Weyl group
N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})/{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
and one calculates that
(N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}))^{n}
is the four element group generated by
n
and
t_{0}
. On the other hand, preserving the notation of the above lemma, one checks that since
n_{a}
is fixed by
n_{a}
we have that
y^{1}_{a}
can be taken to be
1
, and
n_{a}^{1}=n_{a}\check{a}(y^{1}_{a})=n_{a}
. Consequently
\langle n^{1}_{a}\colon a\in\tilde{\Pi}\rangle=\{1,n\}
is a Tits group and is a proper subgroup of
(N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}))^{n}
.

B.2.
Results about
n

Lemma B.2.1
.

For all
t\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
we have that

n^{2}=(nt)^{2}
is central.

Proof.

Fix
t\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
. From Lemma
B.1.1
we know that
n^{2}=(nt)^{2}
.
It will be enough to show that for all
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
we have
{{}^{(nt)^{2}}\!}X^{t}_{a}=X^{t}_{a}
.

Fix
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
.
Since
{{}^{nt}\!}n^{t}_{a}=n^{t}_{a}
and
\Ad({(nt)^{2}})\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})=\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})
, we have

N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})\,\bigcap\,\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})\exp(X^{t}_{a})\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})=N_{\tilde{G}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})\,\bigcap\,\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K})\exp({{}^{(nt)^{2}}\!}X^{t}_{a})\mathbf{U}_{-a}(\tilde{K}).

A calculation shows this can happen if and only if
{{}^{(nt)^{2}}\!}X^{t}_{a}=X^{t}_{a}
.
∎

Corollary B.2.2
.

If
n
has order four, then for all
t\in{\tilde{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}
we have
n^{2}=(nt)^{2}=\lambda_{n}(-1)
.

Proof.

Recall from Section
3.3
that there is an
h\in\tilde{G}
such that
h^{-1}nh=\lambda_{n}(i)
. Thus,

\lambda_{n}(-1)=(h^{-1}nh)(h^{-1}nh)=h^{-1}n^{2}h.

Since
n^{2}
is central, the conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma B.2.3
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-quasi-split,
K
-split, then we may assume
{\Fro}(n)=n
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{G}
is
k
-quasi-split and
K
-split, then we may assume
\{X_{a}\}_{a\in\tilde{\Pi}}
is a Chevalley-Steinberg system for
\mathbf{G}
(see
[
25
, Section 2.9]
). Note that we then have
{\Fro}(n_{a})=n_{{\Fro}(a)}
for all
a\in\tilde{\Pi}
.

If
w\in\tilde{W}
has a reduced decomposition
w=s_{a_{1}}\cdots s_{a_{r}}
where
a_{i}\in\tilde{\Pi}
, then the lift
n_{w}
of
w
to
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
defined by
n_{w}=n_{a_{1}}\cdots n_{a_{r}}
is independent of the reduced decomposition of
w

[
25
, Proposition 2.9.11]
. It thus follows that if
-1\in\tilde{W}
has a reduced decomposition
-1=s_{a_{1}}\cdots s_{a_{m}}
, then
{\Fro}(n)={\Fro}(n_{a_{1}}\cdots n_{a_{m}})=n_{{\Fro}(a_{1})}\cdots n_{{\Fro}(a_{m})}=n
since
s_{{\Fro}(a_{1})}\cdots s_{{\Fro}(a_{m})}
is another reduced decomposition of
-1
. ∎

Remark B.2.4
.

If
\mathbf{G}
is not quasi-split, then it may be the case that
n
cannot be Frobenius fixed. As an example, let
D
be a central division algebra over
k
of index
2
and let
\mathbf{G}=\mathrm{SL}_{1,D}
. Then our
n
fixes exactly one vertex of the Bruhat-Tits building
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
while
{\Fro}
fixes exactly the barycenter
x
of an alcove
C
. This latter fact follows from the fact that since
\{x\}
is the underlying set of
{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbf{G},k)
, the reductive quotient
\mathsf{G}_{x}
is defined over
{\mathfrak{f}}
and therefore is quasi-split by Lang’s theorem. And so a Borel subgroup
\mathsf{B}\subset\mathsf{G}_{x}
defined over
{\mathfrak{f}}
is a torus since
\mathbf{G}
is anisotropic, whence
\mathsf{G}_{x}
is an
{\mathfrak{f}}
-torus. This implies that
x
is an interior point of
C
(necessarily the barycenter). As a consequence one concludes that
n
cannot be Frobenius fixed.

B.3.
Results about
n_{F}

Recall that
n_{\Fro}
and
n_{F}={{}^{\lambda_{n}^{-1}(\pi)}\!}n_{\Fro}
are defined in Definitions
7.4.1
and
7.5.1
.
By construction,
n_{F}\in N_{\mathbf{G}(K_{n})_{x_{0},0}}({\mathbf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}})
normalizes
N^{n\lambda_{n}(\xi)}
. In this section we assume
\mathbf{G}
is
K
-split,
k
-quasi-split and simply connected.

From Lemma
B.2.3
, we may and do assume
{\Fro}(n)=n
.
From Lemma
7.5.3
there exists
z\in Z_{0}=\tilde{Z}_{0}
such that

{\Fro}\left[N_{q}(nz\lambda_{n}(\xi))\right]=n_{F}^{-1}(nz\lambda_{n}(\xi))n_{F}
. Since
-1\in W
, the center of
\mathbf{G}
is an elementary abelian two group and so

z^{2}=1
. Since
Z_{0}=Z_{0^{+}}
and
p\neq 2
, we conclude that
z=1
. Thus
{\Fro}\left[N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(\xi))\right]=n_{F}^{-1}(n\lambda_{n}(\xi))n_{F}
.

Lemma B.3.1
.

We have
n_{F}\in N^{n\lambda_{n}(-1)}
.

Proof.

Since
n^{2}
is central, we have
\Ad(n)\lambda_{n}(\xi)\cdot\Ad(n^{2})\lambda_{n}(\xi)
is trivial. Thus,
N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(\xi))=\Ad(n^{q})(\lambda_{n}(\xi))\cdot n^{q}
.

Suppose first that
n^{2}=1
. In this case we have
\xi=-1
, and so

N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(-1))=\Ad(n^{q})(\lambda_{n}(-1))\cdot n^{q}=\Ad(n)\lambda_{n}(-1)\cdot n=\lambda_{n}(-1)n=n\Ad(n^{-1})\lambda_{n}(-1)
. Thus,
from Corollary
7.5.3
we conclude that

n\lambda_{n}(-1)=n\lambda_{n}(-1)={\Fro}(N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(-1))=(n_{F})^{-1}\cdot(n\lambda_{n}(-1))\cdot n_{F}.

Consequently,
n_{F}\in N^{n\lambda_{n}(-1)}
.

Suppose now that
n
has order four. In this case we can take
\xi=i
, and so

N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(i))=\Ad(n^{q})(\lambda_{n}(i))\cdot n^{q}
. We have two cases:
q\equiv 1\,(4)
and
q\equiv 3\,(4)

Suppose first that
q\equiv 1\,(4)
.
In this case
{\Fro}(i)=i
and

N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(i))=\Ad(n)(\lambda_{n}(i))\cdot n=n\lambda_{n}(i)
.
Thus, from Corollary
7.5.3
we conclude that

n\lambda_{n}(i)=n\lambda_{n}(i)={\Fro}[N_{q}((n\lambda_{n}(i)))]=(n_{F})^{-1}\cdot(n\lambda_{n}(i))\cdot n_{F},

and so
n_{F}\in N^{n\lambda_{n}(i)}
.

Suppose now that
q\equiv 3\,(4)
. In this case
{\Fro}(i)=-i
and

N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(i))=\Ad(n^{3})(\lambda_{n}(i))\cdot n^{3}
. From Lemma
B.2.1
and Corollary
B.2.2
,
n^{2}=\lambda_{n}(-1)
is central, so

N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(i))=\Ad(n)\lambda_{n}(i)\cdot n\lambda_{n}(-1)=\lambda_{n}(-i)n\lambda_{n}(-1)=n\cdot\Ad(n^{-1})\lambda_{n}(-i)\cdot\lambda_{n}(-1)=n\lambda_{n}(-i).

Thus,
from Corollary
7.5.3
we conclude that

n\lambda_{n}(i)={\Fro}[n\lambda_{n}(-i)]={\Fro}[N_{q}(n\lambda_{n}(i))]=(n_{F})^{-1}\cdot(n\lambda_{n}(i))\cdot n_{F},

and so,
n_{F}\in N^{n\lambda_{n}(i)}
.
∎

The following result now follows from the above work and Corollaries
7.4.11
and
7.5.5
.

Corollary B.3.2
.

Suppose
\mathbf{G}
is a simply connected,
K
-split,
k
-quasi-split group and
-1
belongs to
\tilde{W}
. Let
n\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}
be a lift of
-1
such that
{\Fro}(n)=n
. The set of
k
-stable classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
is indexed by the conjugacy classes in
\tilde{W}
.
If
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is a
k
-torus in the conjugacy-class corresponding to
w^{\prime}\in\tilde{W}
, then, up to
G^{{\Fro}}
-conjugacy, the set of
k
-embeddings of
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
into
\mathbf{G}
is indexed by
\tilde{\mathsf{A}}^{\!\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\displaystyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\textstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.67998pt}{\resizebox{471860}{393216}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94443pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#\mathstrut$}}}}}}{}^{-1}_{w^{\prime}{\Fro}}
. Finally, the set of
\mathbf{G}(k)
-classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
is indexed by
N^{n\lambda_{n}(\xi)}_{\sim Fr}
.
∎

Remark B.3.3
.

This Corollary and Lemma
B.1.3
were used in Example
7.10
to compute the number of rational classes in
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}^{k}_{T}
for
\mathbf{T}
corresponding to
-1
in the Weyl group of
{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
. We also used this Corollary to double-check the results about the tori that correspond to
-1
for
\SL_{2}
and
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
in Examples
7.7
and
7.8
.
